Subgoal-aware Memory Graph for GUI Agent

GUI agents aim to automate complex user tasks by interacting with mobile interfaces, but ef- fective decision-making in long-horizon set- tings requires reusing prior experience rather than relying solely on the environment ob- servations. Existing memory-based methods typically store experience either at the single- action level or as entire trajectories. Single- step actions are often too fine-grained to carry meaningful semantics, since the success of an individual action does not necessarily imply progress towards the overall goal. In contrast, whole trajectories are often too coarse-grained and difficult to reuse across different tasks. We propose a subgoal-aware memory graph that distils past trajectories into structured, reusable evidence at the level of subgoals. We define a subgoal as consecutive actions shar- ing a single intent, the granularity at which progress toward the task becomes meaning- ful. To reduce redundancy and support cross- task reuse, we organise subgoal experiences as nodes in a graph, where related memories can be merged, extended, and retrieved based on the environment observation. On SPA-Bench, our method improves success rate by 11.11% over the baseline.
---
Superintelligent Software Engineering: Governance That Scales with Capability

Software agents are being trained to exceed human engineers; we argue that capability alone does not produce better software engineering. We define superintelligent software engineering as the discipline of building and evolving software with agents whose capability exceeds that of human engineers, in which governance (gates, contracts, decisions and decay control) scales with capability. The argument rests on three claims. First, correctness in a project rests on decisions and contracts that the code does not carry. Second, knowledge decays. Third, once an agent outpaces its reviewers, reading catches a shrinking share of the changes built on stale knowledge. A simple model shows that, past a fixed review capacity, escaping changes grow linearly with throughput while the share that reading catches falls toward zero; governance can lower the slope, capability alone cannot. An exploratory snapshot of 452 of the most-starred public GitHub repositories finds a root-level or .github agent context file in 48.67% (95% confidence interval 44.1 to 53.3); in the median file that names repository paths, 9.5% to 22% of those paths do not resolve, depending on how a reference is read. We then present OSTEON, a discipline for the regime, released with a DOI (10.5281/zenodo.23436020): each project gets a governed knowledge repository of decisions and contracts, every change is checked mechanically against it, and the knowledge itself is checked for decay at every session start; planning is gated, and sessions record what they learn without writing rules. We report no measured effect of OSTEON, mark claims about its mechanisms as opinion and the author's practice heuristics as design rationale, and state the questions that would falsify the thesis.

The Value of Memory in Language Agents

Memory enables language agents to carry useful distinctions from past experience into future decisions. Its value depends on which distinctions survive, whether a bounded agent can use them, and what can be recovered or revised as demands evolve. We develop a decision-theoretic framework that follows this value from raw history through persistent state, operational use, and costly recovery and revision. Original and retained value are expressed in units of avoided task loss; risk-cost frontiers describe attainable recovery and preservation across uses. Unknown demand yields value profiles, while interactive feedback changes the distribution of future work. A cost-aware simulation condition and five minimal counterexamples expose the roles of information timing, retrieval access, component interaction, feedback and demand uncertainty. In particular, a pointer with zero mutual information about the answer can still reduce the cost of exact recovery. Diagnostics on LoCoMo, a two-project update task and longitudinal cases exhibit failures of preservation, use and measurement. At fixed retrieved evidence, a saved ledger changes subsequent answers; at fixed agent outputs, an evaluator revision changes scores. The framework connects these distinctions to operational risk bounds and testable principles for allocating memory, preserving recovery options and revising evidence dependencies.

RecallGS: Geometry-Grounded Recall for Online Semantic Gaussian Splatting

Online semantic 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables open-vocabulary scene understanding from streaming observations. However, existing methods recover geometric context missing from image-derived semantic features using an additionally learned 3D network, incurring extra training and runtime overhead. We observe that such geometric-semantic evidence may already exist in the agent's observation history and can be retrieved rather than learned. Based on this insight, we introduce RecallGS (Geometry-Grounded Recall for Online Semantic Gaussian Splatting), a lightweight, training-free mechanism that treats past observations as a non-parametric geometric-semantic prior. RecallGS follows a recall-ground-fuse pipeline: CLIP retrieves relevant observations, alpha-compositing weights identify geometrically supported views, and aggregate their semantic features over each Gaussian's rendered footprint. On ScanNet and ScanNet++, RecallGS achieves strong semantic segmentation performance at 2.48 FPS, demonstrating that geometric-semantic context can be effectively recovered from observation history without an additional learned 3D branch.

Beyond Accuracy: Disentangling Decomposition from Typed-State Quality Assurance in Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines

Multi-agent LLM pipelines are often credited with gains in software engineering tasks, but it is rarely clear whether a gain stems from the typed contract between agents or from the specialized prompts that decomposition enables. We study this attribution problem in Requirements Engineering, comparing three architectures for classifying requirements as functional or non-functional and then prioritizing them with MoSCoW: a single-call baseline; a two-call variant that reuses the pipeline's specialized prompts but passes an unvalidated intermediate; and a pipeline with a Pydantic-validated typed state. The 18 conditions cover PROMISE NFR+ (625 requirements), three locally hosted small language models, and two languages. The F1 gain over the baseline is associated with the two-call, specialized-prompt variant (up to +0.075), whereas the typed state adds a small difference (-0.003 to +0.013) that is significant in one of six comparisons. Beyond accuracy, the typed pipeline contains out-of-range model outputs at the agent boundary, while the unvalidated variant fails later and drops the requirement, and it shows a small, reproducible increase in inter-model MoSCoW consistency whose cause our design cannot isolate. The study is associative: the contrasts bundle prompts, call count, and staged context, and the results come from three 7--8B models on one dataset. The implementation and replication package are archived on Zenodo (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23380444).

CoWriter: Guiding Methodological and Synergetic Human-LLM Co-Authoring

While LLMs have transformed text generation, standard chat-and-canvas interfaces often reduce human authoring to quite passive supervision, causing review fatigue, over-reliance on AI, and the sidelining of the human role in long-form, source-grounded writing. Prior systems typically address isolated writing phases, leaving comprehensive end-to-end human-LLM collaboration largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce CoWriter, a proactive agentic scheme that adapts expert human (co-)authoring methodologies from cognitive science to flexibly steer the entire writing workflow while actively encouraging the user to engage. In an exploratory user study, compared to a standard LLM baseline, authors using CoWriter better expressed their perspectives and maintained a higher sense of document ownership, supported quantitatively by $2.2\times$ more successful agent engagements. Furthermore, external reader judges rated the resulting texts higher in "human touch" qualities like storytelling and integrating unique personal experiences. Overall, our results demonstrate that proactively engaging users through established human practices is beneficial for effective LLM-supported co-authoring, providing useful methodologies, techniques and insights for future research on collaborative agents.

RIPPLE: Recovery Internalization through Process-Level Policy Learning and Effect-Aware Credit for Long-Horizon Agentic Coding

Long-horizon coding agents can observe a failure and generate a plausible critique yet continue along the same incorrect trajectory. We call this the Critique-to-Action Handoff Gap. We introduce RIPPLE, a recovery-oriented training framework that internalizes process-level guidance into a single self-recovering policy. A prefix-only teacher provides evidence-grounded recovery supervision without access to future actions or terminal outcomes. After lightweight distillation, reinforcement learning assigns stage-specific credit to reflection triggering, diagnosis, and corrective action. Local validity signals gate trajectory advantages so that intermediate recovery decisions do not indiscriminately inherit terminal outcomes. Across four repository-level software-engineering benchmarks, RIPPLE improves the base policy by an arithmetic mean of 4.8 score points, with no auxiliary critic at deployment. Controlled ablations isolate the benefits of local, stage-aligned credit, and matched-prefix interventions show that critique semantics affect subsequent correction. Recovery gains concentrate where the prefix contains sufficient diagnostic evidence and the repository state remains reversible within the remaining budget.

PointerBench: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Fine-Grained GUI Grounding

Multimodal Large Language Models have shown promising performance in computer-use tasks, from planning user workflows to grounding natural-language actions to precise UI targets. Existing end-to-end benchmarks primarily emphasize long-horizon task completion, while GUI grounding benchmarks remain focused on conventional controls and a limited set of professional interfaces. We introduce POINTERBENCH, a diagnostic suite of 1,500 screenshot-instruction pairs probing three complementary capabilities: thin sub-element identification and geometric grounding in spreadsheets (POINTERBENCH-Sheets), character-level grounding in long-text documents (POINTERBENCH-Text), and mixed GUI grounding across 100 professional applications (POINTERBENCH-Pro). We carefully curate each task with pixel-exact target geometry and evaluate both general-purpose multimodal models and 39 specialist GUI grounding configurations. We find text grounding to be the most challenging setting, with Claude Fable 5, GPT-5.6 Sol, and the leading native specialist UI-Venus-2-9B achieving 69.6%, 53.4%, and 25.8% success on POINTERBENCH-Text despite respective 83.2%, 77.7%, and 54.5% average success across POINTERBENCH. These results reveal substantial headroom in fine-grained GUI grounding across frontier and specialist models. We publicly release the benchmark and evaluation code at https://github.com/warmwindOS/pointerbench.

Memory-Aware Reward Modeling: Learning Human Preferences for LLM Memory Use

Memory enables language model assistants to maintain continuity across sessions and generate personalized responses, but knowing more about a user does not necessarily produce responses they prefer. Existing evaluations assess memory capabilities without directly establishing whether memory use benefits the user. To study this gap, we collect approximately 1,000 pairwise judgments from 87 participants using their own ChatGPT queries and personal memories. We find that memory-conditioned responses are not consistently preferred over responses generated without memory and in some cases, users strongly prefer the latter. Automatic evaluators also show limited agreement with users and favor memory-conditioned responses substantially more often than users do. Motivated by these gaps, we introduce a memory-aware reward model that learns from user judgments, achieving 72.8\% preference prediction accuracy and outperforming the existing baselines. Our findings motivate evaluation and optimization objectives that align memory use with the preferences of the people whose information is being used.

Notation3: Graph Terms for RDF

Notation3 Logic was introduced as an extension of RDF allowing the user to express rules on top of triples and to make statements about graphs. The original proposal of the logic only came with an informal specification of its semantics which has been refined later on. While this refinement clarified the meaning of rules and implicitly quantified variables, it left the meaning of graphs used as terms - so-called graph terms - open. In this paper we aim to close this gap. We discuss in detail, how graph terms are used in Notation3, which formal requirements they need to meet, and provide examples to illustrate the difficulties when defining their semantics. We then introduce an abstract syntax for the logic and define its semantics in two steps: similarly to RDF's simple interpretations, we define interpretations and basic entailment for Notation3 which covers plain RDF triples but also triples containing graph terms. N3-entailment is then an extension of basic entailment which assigns meaning to the different special predicates of the logic like for example the implication symbol. N3-entailment is defined in a way that it can be extended whenever a new predicate is added to the logic. As Notation3 Logic is currently under development, we expect many predicates to be added. Our research provides and important step towards the full standardisation of the logic.

Can Entanglement-Induced Feature-Map Designs Improve Projected Quantum Kernels?

Projected quantum kernels (PQKs) have emerged as a notable approach to circumvent the hardness of machine learning tasks arising from the exponential dimensionality of the quantum Hilbert space. However, recent PQK studies often rely heavily on extensive classical hyperparameter tuning of the radial basis function (RBF) kernel parameter and the support vector machine regularization parameter. The extensive tuning can mask the true representational power of the underlying PQKs induced by quantum feature maps, which has not been explored in detail. Thus, this work aims to expose the structural capabilities of PQKs by isolating and evaluating their native inductive bias through comparisons between untuned and tuned PQKs against the RBF baseline. Furthermore, we propose novel quantum feature maps that leverage two-qubit entanglement to capture multivariate correlations in IoT data. The results show that extensive tuning can lead to competitive performance architectures, while our proposed entanglement topologies exhibit a significantly stronger native inductive bias. This reveals that superior quantum architectural design can yield structurally better PQKs with improvements arising from quantum-mechanical properties rather than from classical hyperparameter tuning.

Reducing Multi-Horizon Photovoltaic Forecast Error through Tree-Anchored Expert Combination

A strong direct tree model may remain the best standalone photovoltaic forecaster, yet a weighted combination can reduce its residual errors. We examine whether complementary temporal predictors can lower the tree's mean absolute error (MAE) over 15--240 minutes without weaker ensemble members degrading performance. The candidate bank contains LightGBM and seven neural implementations with different temporal representations. A combiner fitted to MAE has nonnegative weights that sum to one. Unhelpful candidates can receive zero weight, while the tree anchors the combination and provides a development-stage fallback. The main design shares weights across horizons; horizon-specific weights and alternative losses test whether further complexity is needed. On 56,078 windows from two co-located public PVDAQ systems, the historical shared-MAE policy reduces scaled MAE by 3.21\% relative to the tree.
  The latest bounded neural correction does not reduce MAE further than this static combination. The results support selective combination of complementary predictions in this bank rather than equal averaging or indiscriminate addition of modules. Both analyses reuse a previously inspected evaluation period. They do not establish independent replication, weather-specific causation or universal gains from eight experts.

Reward-Weighted Preference Optimization for Selective Combinatorial Optimization Problems

Reinforcement learning has emerged as an effective approach for combinatorial optimization, but most existing methods focus on problems such as the Travelling Salesperson Problem (TSP) in which all elements must be served. Selective combinatorial optimization problems (SCOPs), including the Orienteering Problem (OP), Prize-Collecting TSP (PCTSP), Stochastic PCTSP and Knapsack Problem (KP), additionally require selecting which elements to include. This selection creates sampled solutions with substantially different objective values, yet standard preference optimization reduces such comparisons to binary rankings and discards the magnitude of these differences.
  We introduce Reward-Weighted Preference Optimization (RWPO), a pairwise training objective that incorporates reward-gap information into preference learning. For candidate trajectories sampled from the same instance, RWPO weights each preference loss according to the normalized difference in their rewards, assigning greater training emphasis to comparisons that represent larger improvements in solution quality. We further combine RWPO with scale-specific fine-tuning and reward- and likelihood-weighted tilted sampling to adapt trained policies to larger and shifted instance distributions without relying on local-search post-processing.
  Experiments on the OP, PCTSP, Stochastic PCTSP, and KP show that RWPO consistently improves over unweighted preference optimization and policy-gradient baselines across a wide range of instance sizes. On the OP, the resulting solver exceeds the mean reward of the Compass metaheuristic at scales from 100 to 2,000 nodes. The results demonstrate that preserving reward-gap information can substantially improve preference-based learning for selective combinatorial optimization and support generalization to large-scale instances.

QICOM: Open-Source Software for Reproducible Image Quality Evaluation, Blinded Assessment, and Radiomic Comparison

QICOM (Quality Image COMparator) is open-source software for quantitative image comparison, paired radiomics, and blinded assessment. It saves processing settings, image identities, measurements, and response mappings so a comparison can be reopened and recalculated. We evaluate reference agreement, responses to controlled perturbations, and recovery of saved analyses. Synthetic experiments cover 37 quantitative descriptors using independentnumerical references and separate upstream integration comparisons. The QICOM/MIRP backend meets the reference tolerances for all 482 numeric IBSI 1 digital-phantom entries and all 1,622 entries across configurations A-E of the public CT reference case; these comprise 1,804 feature/aggregation comparisons and 300 preprocessing diagnostics. Restricting masked SSIM and HFEN to complete kernel support removes the observed dependence on excluded pixels. In a synthetic filtering example, stronger smoothing increases SSIM while worsening MSE and HFEN relative to milder smoothing and changing GLCM contrast more strongly. Reopened radiomics projects reproduce all 43 feature pairs per comparison, and two blinded deliveries return four correctly mapped scripted responses. These experiments document the calculations and data transfers linking the three workflows.

Trion-1: Unifying Language, Protein Sequence, and Structure with Diffusion Language Models

We envision a general-purpose protein foundation model as a unified, interactive model of sequence, structure, and function, capable of understanding, generation, and cross-modal translation while retaining the instruction-following and reasoning capabilities of modern LLMs. Toward this vision, we introduce Trion-1, the first native, unified, multimodal diffusion language model (dLLM) for proteins. Trion-1 is built on two key design choices: (1) a unified discrete token space that jointly represents sequence, structure, and natural language; (2) a dLLM backbone that replaces strict left-to-right factorization with bidirectional or semi-bidirectional token interactions, enabling global residue communication and long-range contact modeling that are naturally suited to structure modeling. This formulation unifies six core capabilities: folding, inverse folding, sequence understanding, structure understanding, sequence design, and structure design, while retaining the native language capabilities of general-purpose LLMs. On established benchmarks, Trion-1 achieves broadly competitive performance. In folding, Trion-1 achieves a TM-score above 0.8 with a single sample under strict homology control, matching or exceeding the existing tri-modal model using 200 samples. For sequence understanding, Trion-1 performs on par with top-tier LLMs such as Claude Opus 4.7 (62.37%). These results support well-aligned sequence-structure-language representations and strong cross-modal generative capabilities.

Predicting Music Valence and Arousal via Textual and Multimodal In-Context Learning in Large Language Models

While arousal can be reliably predicted from musical audio, emotional valence remains difficult to model due to its subjective and context-dependent nature. In this work, we investigate the potential of in-context learning (ICL) with large language models to advance continuous valence and arousal prediction. Rather than predicting emotion scores directly, our framework leverages contextual demonstrations from an auxiliary dataset to refine the initial predictions produced by a small, task-specific base model. We systematically evaluate a text-based ICL variant using lyrics and generated audio captions, alongside a multimodal variant that incorporates raw audio signals. Using the entire MER60 dataset strictly as a held-out test set, our experiments demonstrate substantial performance gains, particularly for valence. Text-based ICL improves the valence $R^2$ from 0.110 to 0.427 with 80 demonstration shots, while the multimodal variant achieves 0.346 with only 10 shots. These findings suggest that training-free ICL offers an effective inference-time strategy for current language models to predict continuous music emotion.

Different Tasks, Different Timesteps: Temporal Task-Aware Quantization for Diffusion Language Models

Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) introduce unique quantization challenges due to their iterative denoising process. In this work, we reveal that quantization sensitivity is both taskand time-dependent, with different downstream tasks exhibiting distinct precision requirements across denoising steps. We further show that these patterns can be predicted without groundtruth labels or task-specific evaluation metrics. Building on these findings, we introduce T 2Quant, a task and time-aware mixedprecision quantization framework that uses a label-free sensitivity signal to optimally allocate activation precision across denoising steps under a fixed bit-width budget. Extensive experiments across three DLMs and six benchmarks demonstrate that T 2Quant consistently improves the average accuracy of existing quantization methods, achieving gains of up to 6.7 percentage points under the same budget.

When More Metadata Misleads: Probing Field-Level Grounding in LLM-Generated Museum Descriptions

Metadata-conditioned generation is often framed as a data-to-text task in which additional input fields should improve grounding. Museum description generation challenges this assumption because catalogue records are heterogeneous, partially visual, and unevenly documented across geographic regions. We study how metadata composition shapes the generation of artefact descriptions through a two-phase framework. Phase 1 analyses natural metadata variation in a large museum collection, where field count, field type, image availability, and Place co-vary. Phase 2 introduces controlled field probes that hold object identity fixed while varying metadata quantity, field type, and image access. Our results show that metadata quantity alone does not explain generation behaviour. Field type determines whether models ground, omit, or infer information, while images can both support visual grounding and increase unsupported interpretation. More broadly, our findings show that metadata-conditioned data-to-text evaluation should assess field- and claim-level support, rather than relying only on aggregate input coverage or reference similarity.

Prompting Strategy Effects Depend on the Judge: A Multilingual Study of LLM-as-a-Judge Robustness

LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation is increasingly applied to multilingual tasks, yet prompting strategies such as Chain-of-Thought and Role Conditioning are recommended based solely on English benchmarks. Whether their effectiveness transfers across judge models and languages remains unknown: prompt-sensitivity studies are largely English-centric, while existing multilingual judge studies fix the prompt template. In this work, we address this gap by measuring this interaction through a benchmark of 9 prompting strategies, 4 open-weight judges, 27 languages, and 4 tasks across 13 datasets, yielding over 1M pointwise scores on gold-reference outputs, validated against human judgments. We find that prompting strategies are not transferable: the choice of the judge model dominates the choice of prompting strategy, strategy effects reverse direction across judges, and stability degrades in low-resource languages, where these effects are concealed by aggregate reporting. These findings show that prompting effects in multilingual evaluation are conditioned on the judge, task, and language. Consequently, prompting strategies should be validated for the intended evaluation setting, and cross-study comparisons should account for both judge and prompt configuration.

Network topology shapes consensus and vulnerability in LLM populations

As large language models (LLMs) are deployed in interacting populations, their collective behaviour depends not only on the models but also on who interacts with whom. Studies of convention formation in LLM populations have mainly tested well-mixed interactions. We place policies extracted from four LLMs on fully connected, random regular, and heterogeneous networks, with $10^3$ to $10^5$ agents playing the naming game. Topology affects how quickly consensus emerges, sometimes whether it emerges at all, and, in rare cases, which convention wins. Sparsest networks slow consensus up to twelvefold. Yet, by preserving correlations between persistent neighbours, they restore consensus in some cases that fail to converge under well-mixed interactions. Networks also govern vulnerability to adversarial attack. We measure the critical mass of committed agents needed to overturn an established convention. Tipping thresholds follow the same ordering across models and conditions: hubs $\leq$ random $\leq$ low-degree nodes. On the most heterogeneous topology, median critical masses are 3%, 16%, and 35%, respectively; targeting hubs requires one-sixth as many committed agents as random placement. For most models, networked populations are easier to tip than well-mixed ones, so well-mixed estimates can overstate robustness. Assessing the safety of multi-agent AI requires mapping who interacts with whom.

Prompt Sensitivity in Tool-Augmented Model Comparisons

Language models are compared on question-answering benchmarks that pose each question in one wording, and a reworded question can change which of two models scores higher. We ask whether giving the models tools makes such comparisons more stable. An accuracy gain cannot answer this, because tools change several steps between the question and the scored answer at once. We propose a matched protocol that separates those steps: the answer readout, reasoning, the decision to call, the tool's answer, and the report supplied without a call. We apply it to six open models in five domains under five wordings. We find that tools raise accuracy where a program answers the question and widen the wording sensitivity of many pairwise comparisons. The widening follows how often a model calls, and call frequency follows the wording for some models and not others. It also depends on the interface and the instructions: a native tool-calling interface and a second tool description leave no detected widening in molecules, and sensitivity persists where tools return intermediate results. These findings show that deterministic reports do not guarantee stable comparisons. We recommend reporting call frequency and comparison stability under several wordings beside accuracy.

PriMa-Causa: Causal Prescriptive Maintenance

Automated manufacturing requires data-driven decisions under uncertainty and sparse observational records, and unplanned downtime is a dominant cost of getting them wrong. Prescriptive maintenance recommends a maintenance action against an impending failure rather than merely predicting it. Established reference architectures couple predictive models with rule- or optimisation-based recommendation logic. Selecting a corrective action, however, requires an interventional quantity: how the line behaviour would change if the action were set. Manufacturing data is observational, so a model fitted to predict the outcome estimates an associational quantity and may misdirect the choice of action without the prediction error revealing it. Causal estimators target the interventional quantity but require an expert-led causal study per case, for which the bounded decision window of operation leaves no room. This dissertation analyses how causal reasoning can be embedded into prescriptive maintenance so that actions are ranked by their estimated interventional effect. The result is PriMa-Causa, built from three components: a causal reasoning layer that integrates the estimate into the PriMa reference model, a parametric simulator that supplies the interventional ground truth absent from manufacturing data, and a graph-conditioned tabular causal foundation model that, pre-trained once on this synthetic production-line prior, answers interventional queries in a single forward pass. PriMa-Causa attains a lower prescription error than associational baselines, with the largest margin under weak overlap and sparse context, and a partially disclosed causal graph reduces this error further. The causal inductive bias of the synthetic prior and the graph conditioning can thus replace per-case estimator fitting, a step toward automating causal inference for autonomous and self-healing maintenance.

Aggregating CNN responses into a compact discriminative vector, given their lack of geometric invariance, is a challenge. We use a scalable retrieval pipeline to show the efficacy of the proposed vector. The increased discriminative power is confirmed by experimental evaluation of our method on two difficult benchmarks (INRIA Holidays, UKBench). The significant improvement in retrieval accuracy over state-of-the-art approaches comes with only a slight dimension increase.

Mixing Matters: Evidence Position Bias Across Sequence Mixers in Long-Context Question Answering

Long-context models often use answer-bearing evidence less effectively when it appears away from a prompt boundary. We compare this position bias across Transformer, state-space, and hybrid Mamba architectures by moving one gold document through a fixed set of nine distractors. The evaluated Transformers show a beginning advantage that the evaluated state-space models lack, while both families favor evidence near the end. A matched pure-versus-hybrid Mamba comparison points in the same direction but remains statistically uncertain. The family difference appears among models that can answer the task. Transformer primacy reappears on synthetic retrieval, where state-space ceiling prevents a family comparison. A corpus swap within one state-space architecture changes overall accuracy without a detectable change in curve shape. Prompt changes can create or nearly remove a beginning advantage. Position remains decodable across architectures even when response curves differ, and attention concentration near initial tokens covaries with beginning sensitivity in the tested Transformer family. These exploratory results show that effective context use depends on the evaluated architecture family, capability, task, and prompt, without isolating the sequence mixer as the sole cause.

Post-Audit Mirage: Identifiability Limits of Offline Agent Verification

Offline tests can fail to reveal whether an agent update will remain safe after deployment. We operationalize this post-audit mirage using matched safe and harmful lifecycles that expose identical verifier-visible evidence while an isolated scorer records opposite deployment truth. Our theorem applies standard observational-equivalence and indistinguishability ideas to this executable audit boundary and states the resulting error-abstention tradeoff. The Post-Audit Mirage benchmark makes that boundary executable across authorization, scheduling, and shared-capacity environments. IdentifiedRangeMonitor uses fresh group-labelled harm outcomes to return deploy, hold, cannot determine, or unsupported. Experiments show that monitoring can recover the missing distinction in favorable settings but may remain undecided for rare groups, small effects, or missing observations. Verifier-agent evaluation must therefore test both calculation validity and whether the available evidence supports the deployment decision.

SetCommit: Coordinated Token Commitments for Few-Step Diffusion Language Model Decoding

Diffusion language models generate multiple token positions in parallel, but few-step decoding makes parallel commitment difficult: tokens that appear reliable individually may be jointly inconsistent, while premature commitments can prevent later context-dependent refinement. In this paper, we introduce SetCommit, a training-free method for coordinated parallel token commitment. Instead of selecting tokens independently by confidence, SetCommit selects a commitment set that balances token reliability against local joint commitment risk, preserving conditioning opportunities for subsequent denoising steps. It further performs budget-neutral local exchanges, replacing vulnerable earlier commitments when newly revealed context supports more reliable alternatives. SetCommit requires no retraining or architectural modification and is compatible with existing diffusion decoding strategies. Experiments with LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B on GSM8K, MATH-500, MBPP, and HumanEval show accuracy/pass@1 gains of up to 7.4 percentage points over native decoding at 32 model evaluations. At common quality targets, geometric-mean incremental speedups range from $1.44$ to $1.74\times$. Code is available at https://github.com/Kissmetothemoon/SetCommit.

Constraint Reasoning and Large Language Models Join Forces: Providing Contrastive Explanations for Real-World Optimisation-Based Applications

We focus on optimisation-based Decision Support Systems in real-world applications. In this context, we aim to provide contrastive explanations that answer users' questions about recommendations based on the solution of an optimisation problem, specifically a Mixed-Integer Program (MIP). Addressing this endeavour is crucial to ensure human oversight of the decisions implemented, as required by international guidelines on trustworthy and responsible automated decision-making systems. We propose a novel methodology that combines constraint reasoning techniques and Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate contrastive explanations for optimisation problems encoded as MIPs. Although LLMs have shown impressive capabilities in Natural Language processing, they can produce incorrect answers due to reasoning limitations. To overcome these limitations, we leverage constraint reasoning techniques to distil the information necessary to produce an explanation, which is then passed as input to an LLM as the so-called graph of reasons. Such a graph captures the semantics and the relationships among constraints of the optimisation problem. Results on real-world scheduling domains show that incorporating constraint reasoning improves explanations generated by the LLM. In particular, using the graph of reasons enables the LLM to produce natural-language explanations that include more relevant reasons while reducing errors compared to explanations generated without this structure.

ProductWebBench: Evaluating Website Continuity Beyond Task Completion

Frontend coding agents are usually graded on one question: did the requested target get built. Real engineering changes carry a second obligation, because the rest of the site has to keep working. We call that second obligation website continuity, and introduce ProductWebBench, a benchmark that scores both on living frontend repositories: 320 Change tasks over existing code and 80 long-horizon Build tasks. Every task freezes a repository snapshot, staged requirements, browser evidence and deterministic checks before evaluation, and enters only once a reference passes every check and plausible-wrong ones fail. A simulated user reveals the requirements one stage at a time, and every check cleared earlier stays armed for the rest of the run. Across 8,737 runs from 13 models the two come apart: of the Change runs passing every requested-work check, 28.2% still fail a continuity check -- a blank route, horizontal overflow, console errors -- and 10.8% push a browser state they had already passed back to failing. Because the same checks also run on the untouched repository, each regression carries exact provenance, not a post-hoc label: on the 586 runs where both readings exist, 18.3% are caught breaking a verified state. Interaction is a further blind spot, failing in 29.5% of runs whose static DOM already passes. ProductWebBench therefore scores a patch against the living system it lands in, not the target alone.

A Grammar of French Civil Cassation: A Ground-Level Dataset and Tool

This paper presents a dataset of 121,536 ground-reasons pairs across 86,464 rulings of the five civil chambers of the French Court of Cassation (2016-2025). An appeal on points of law cannot itself be appealed and often decides a case for good. Understanding how the Court rules is thus crucial and can help lawyers who draft those appeals. Inside a ruling, every ground of appeal (a distinct legal complaint against the appealed decision) has its own answer from the Court. This answer is called the reasons and it belongs to one of a few doctrinal families. Using a deterministic pipeline, we pair each ground with its reasons and attach the pair to its family, according to the established French taxonomy. We obtain a descriptive grammar of civil cassation that shows which grounds succeed, which legal texts they refer to, and how both evolve over the decade. We release the dataset, the code and an interactive consultation tool.

Can AI Co-Design Structures? An AI Agent for Early-Stage Structural Design

Recent advances in large language models and agentic workflows offer a new paradigm to early-stage structural design translating design intent into structural models. Although text-to-3D approaches have demonstrated that natural language can be used to produce visual, mesh-based geometric models, structural design requires domain-specific geometric arrangements and semantically rich data types. This paper introduces a proof-of-concept workflow for prompt-to-structure generation using an AI agent connected to a COMPAS-based structural modelling API through a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server. Through controlled modelling operations, the agent creates and modifies a graph-based structural data model composed of explicit building elements. The resulting artifacts can be inspected, modified, serialised, and further connected to downstream AEC workflows. The approach is demonstrated through selected prompt-to-structure examples and by progressive and editable design sequences that resemble typical iterative structural design workflows. The results are preliminary, but demonstrate the possibility of combining natural language, agentic workflows, and domain-specific structural data structures as a first step toward AI-assisted structural co-design.

Who Is the Influencer? Consensus Dynamics in Multi-Agent Systems

Multi-agent LLM systems are increasingly used in practice, but their behavior is complex and hard to understand. We design a simple task to ask which model has the most influence on the final consensus when several models are mixed in one system. Experimentally, across seven model families, we find that the opinions of the other models are absorbed into the opinion of the stronger model, the one with more parameters. This happens even though the task requires no pretraining knowledge and has no correct answer. To understand this phenomenon, we interpret the agents as weighted particles moving on a simplex. Larger models carry a larger weight and therefore occupy a deeper well in the energy landscape. A large weight means strong in-context learning: a model with a large weight amplifies the majority of its context. Larger models are generally better at in-context learning, and this is what makes the larger model the influencer. We hope that this work helps to interpret multi-agent systems, whose behavior is becoming too complex for humans to understand.

DeepOpen: Decision-Oriented Intent Classification on BANKING77 and CLINC150

We present DeepOpen, a decision-Oriented intent-classification framework with dataset- specific systems for BANKING77 and CLINC150. On BANKING77, a single English classi- fier continued with sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) achieves 94.1234% test accuracy (2,899/3,080). This is the best observed result among three fixed SAM seeds, selected for display after test evaluation; their mean is 94.0368%. On the 150 in-scope CLINC150 classes, probability fusion of one Laya specialist and three DeBERTa-v3-large classifiers achieves 98.0222% (4,411/4,500). We describe the two configurations, reproduce the reference com- parisons displayed in the accompanying evaluation explorer, and separate headline scores from evidence about improvement. Under a uniform BANKING77 evaluation path, the SAM mean exceeds the historical checkpoint by 0.0433 percentage points, with a shared-row 95% interval of [-0.2273, +0.3247]. Thus, the continuation does not establish a reliable accuracy gain. The report documents strong observed task-specific performance, selection effects, and unresolved historical execution-path differences, rather than a new backbone or a unified algorithmic advantage.

Solving Flood-It Puzzles: A Comparison of Tailored A* Search with Declarative Solver Approaches

We present an optimized approach for finding minimal move sequences to solve Flood-It puzzles using A* search and compare it with approaches using SAT, Integer Programming, and Constraint Programming solvers. The performance of A* search is considerably improved by using an appropriate heuristic and sophisticated equivalences of game states to limit the search space. Each aspect is systematically evaluated, providing insights that generalize to other problems. By comparing the best variant of each approach, the A* search is the fastest in finding a minimal solution in about 75 % of the Flood-It instances tested. This result is in line with our expectation since A* specifically looks for a minimum solution. In contrast, the SAT-based approach is designed to solve the decision problem of whether a solution with a given length exists. An iterative search is required to determine a minimum solution length using a SAT-based approach. However, with increasing complexity, quantified by the minimum solution length, the SAT-based approach outperforms A*. We also compared the different approaches to the decision problem, where A* again performed best for the largest number of puzzles. Here, again, SAT supersedes A* for higher complexities, albeit with a lower crossing point.

Co-Recursive Semantic Self-Extension: A Mathematical Foundation for Open-Ended Intelligence

Artificial intelligence has advanced through scale, data, and computation, yet remains bounded by its representational framework. We ask what makes intelligence open-ended. Every inferential system selects meaningful distinctions, limiting what it can learn. Within a fixed purpose, performance reaches a ceiling set by its semantic domain. Teleological succession, in which purposes arise from earlier ones, faces a similar limit: Boolean-closed purpose-transformations exhaust their reachable semantic territory. We shift from purposes to inferential systems. Drawing on Kant and Gödel, we model cognitive states as partial evaluations over a formal language. Unresolved propositions may be undecided or undecidable. Reasoning can resolve the former but not the latter, leaving a frontier for extension. Non-trivial reductions of undecidable propositions expose undecidable intermediate premises whose reflective reification supports multiple decisive extensions. Finite trace-guided generation remains fallible, requiring meta-level selection and polycontextural exploration. Using Kolmogorov complexity, we show that no architecture with a fixed state description, transition mechanism, and initial condition can generate histories incorporating irreducible algorithmic information. Because physical systems are fixed in this sense, we examine collectives. Coupling finitely many finite agents does not remove the bound. Extension requires persistent external memory preserving semantic content and historical coordinates. Collective generative capacity depends on balancing overlap for transfer with difference for generation. We propose semantic self-extension as a design principle: intelligence should not merely resolve ambiguity within existing meanings, but transform unresolved structures into new spaces of meaning, generating new distinctions, questions, representations, and purposes.
---
What Pairwise Evaluation Misses: Reward Scores across Prompts

Reward models score responses for preference optimization, filtering and ranking, and pooling scores across prompts needs them to be comparable. Yet pairwise evaluation compares two responses to the same prompt, so any score component shared by a prompt's responses cancels and goes untested. We study this component by comparing between-prompt variation in model scores with the same variation in human ratings of the same responses. We propose a human-referenced audit, built from a between-prompt variance share, a sibling-rating regression and prompt substitutions, and apply it to eighteen released scorers and six matched training conditions. We find that scores cluster by prompt more than the ratings do: on HelpSteer2, six reward models have between-prompt shares of 0.34 to 0.64 against a human share near zero. Replacing the original prompt with a fixed instruction keeps substantial clustering but weakens agreement with prompt-level human ratings, so clustering alone does not show agreement with humans. Part of that variation is useful: subtracting prompt means lowers the helpfulness of globally selected responses, and scores stay sensitive to a wrong prompt even when training shuffles prompt means. We propose that reward evaluation check between-prompt variation, sensitivity to a wrong prompt and usefulness for selection separately.

MQAScore: Fine-Grained Text-Music Alignment Evaluation via Large Audio Language Model

Text-to-music alignment is commonly evaluated by the global similarity between text and audio embeddings, which collapses all described attributes into a single score and obscures which ones are missing. We instead introduce MQAScore, which verifies each atomic musical concept along five clip-level dimensions (genre, instrument, mood/theme, vocal, and usage) by the probability that a large audio language model (LALM) answers affirmatively to a binary question. To assess evaluator reliability, we construct verification labels from presence/absence annotations and LLM-guided concept substitutions, and propose the Sensitivity-Invariance Ratio, which measures how selectively an evaluator responds to meaning-changing substitutions. Experiments show that concept-level scoring improves both question-answering and similarity-based evaluators, and MQAScore can be a more reliable and interpretable fine-grained complement to global similarity for evaluating text-to-music alignment.

Dual Exam: Controlled Evaluation of Multimodal Agents and World Models

World Models (WMs) can support Multimodal Agents (MAs) through simulated experience, while Agent trajectories can inform World Model refinement. Evaluating this interaction requires separate evidence about action selection and state evolution. We introduce Dual Exam, which tests Agents under fixed environment response mechanisms and World Models under fixed action interventions across physical, digital, social, and scientific domains. The Agent track compares 10 configurations on 1,088 items in 15 reporting categories. The World Model track contains 400 cases across eight scenarios; 15 configurations have full coverage and one covers six scenarios. GPT 6 Astra leads the case-weighted Agent Index at 31.3, and GPT-Image-2 leads the full-coverage WM Overall at 21.7. These scores use different tasks and metrics and are not directly comparable. A 150-case pilot of one coupled pairing records zero task successes and motivates component-level diagnosis. Results reveal uneven task performance and failures to preserve task-relevant state. Their value for predicting coupled success or sustained reciprocal learning remains to be established.

SCOPE: Compiling Natural-Language Policies into Neurosymbolic Predicate Programs

Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale.

Diagnosing the Explicit-to-Applied Theory of Mind Gap in LLMs

As large language models are deployed in high-stakes roles such as therapy, education, and personal assistance, their social cognition must be assessed reliably. Current Theory of Mind (ToM) benchmarks report aggregate scores that conflate two distinct competencies: explicit ToM (recognizing what someone believes) and applied ToM (using that belief to predict behavior). Building on this distinction, we investigate the gap and its recoverability using four structurally diverse benchmarks. Aggregate reporting obscures uneven competence: on SimpleToM, Llama-3.1-8B scores 51.5 percent overall while recognizing mental states at 99.4 percent and predicting behavior at only 28.9 percent. To assess recovery, we compare structured prompting, matched in-context learning, and our proposed Belief-State Test-Time Training (BSTTT), which adapts lightweight parameters on answered support questions through action reconstruction. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and GPT-oss-20B, recovery depends on the model, task, and intervention. On SimpleToM, reminders combined with BSTTT improve Llama's behavior prediction by 40.8 percentage points; reminders account for most of the overall improvement, with BSTTT adding 1.3 to 5.5 points beyond reminders across models. Contextual examples often outperform weight updates, while BSTTT remains stronger in specific model-benchmark settings. Temporal belief tracking remains difficult despite partial recovery. These findings show that aggregate ToM scores can overstate applied competence while hiding performance recoverable through targeted assistance. We recommend decomposed reporting of explicit and applied accuracy alongside intervention comparisons that establish which failures are recoverable and under what conditions.

Directing to Diversify: Multi-Directional Search for Automatic Heuristic Design

Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong potential to automate heuristic design for NP-hard combinatorial optimization (CO) tasks without human intervention. Recent methods predominantly rely on evolutionary computation (EC) frameworks, where heuristic diversification is mainly induced through EC-like operators or downstream selection, while the direction of heuristic transformation remains largely unharnessed. In this paper, we propose a multi-directional Perturbation-Rewrite Search (PRS) framework for automatic heuristic design that factorizes heuristic transformation through directional search primitives at the generation level. Specifically, PRS operates on heuristic ideas through three complementary directions: expansion toward richer and more expressive designs, compression toward simpler and less specialized designs, and steering for fine-grained refinement, together enabling broader and more sustained exploration of diverse regions in the heuristic design space. PRS further organizes candidate exploration within a breadth-first search-based process with adaptive operator control, which ensures thorough candidate utilization and sustained exploration, and maintains strong search dynamics while preventing premature convergence. Extensive empirical results across diverse CO tasks show that PRS consistently discovers high-quality heuristics, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of explicitly directional exploration design.

Harm Is Behavioural, Moderation Is Lexical: How Language and Culture Decide Whom Automated Safety Systems Protect and Whom They Silence

Content warning: this paper quotes slurs and hateful statements from published test suites; the racial slur among them is masked.
  Automated systems decide at scale which messages are harmful, and they decide unevenly. Classifiers label African American English as offensive up to twice as often as other English, the accounts of prominent drag queens can score as more toxic than those of white nationalists, and safety behaviour that holds in English collapses in low-resource languages. We argue that over-flagging and under-protection share one cause: deciding harm from vocabulary and surface form rather than from what a message does. We organise the evidence into seven failure modes and argue, as testable hypotheses, that harmful behaviours (a request for secrecy, a demand for payment, an attempt to isolate someone) travel across languages better than the words that express them. We propose an evaluation protocol that makes the gap between the best- and worst-served language communities a headline result, and show that the naive gap is inflated under equal service and that its usual bootstrap interval is unreliable. Applied to five open systems in eleven languages, from a keyword lexicon to two current multilingual guard models, the protocol finds that none reaches native-speaker accuracy at separating harmful function from harmless form (51 of 55 system-language pairs fall short); that greater capability raises discrimination in nine languages while leaving Arabic and romanised Hindi near chance, so the gap between the best- and worst-served languages grows with capability; that one system both over-flags and under-protects the same language most; and that a language a system reads worst can look the most fairly treated when only false positives are reported.

Conflict-Aware Logit Adapters for Utility-Preserving Anti-Distillation

While reasoning traces improve model capability, they also provide supervision signals for downstream distillation. Initial efforts to mitigate this issue rely on decoding-time interventions, but they incur high inference overhead and can harm the teacher model's natural fluency. We propose Conflict-Aware Logit Adapters (CALA), a lightweight training-time defense. CALA attaches a small residual adapter to a frozen teacher model and trains only the adapter. A proxy student identifies positions where imitation is most sensitive, and the adapter perturbs the output distribution at these positions while remaining within high-probability regions. A conflict-aware optimization strategy balances anti-distillation and utility objectives, while regularization preserves the teacher's performance and fluency. At inference time, CALA incurs modest overhead and requires no auxiliary model. Experiments on reasoning benchmarks show that CALA substantially reduces successful student imitation while maintaining near-original teacher performance and generation quality. These results establish CALA as a practical and efficient alternative to decoding-time approaches.
---
Algorithmic drift in generative adaptive learning

Generative artificial intelligence promises to democratize personalized tutoring, yet the longitudinal stability of adaptive systems remains under-theorized. Current ethical discourse focuses on static bias. This paper argues that a distinct threat lies in algorithmic drift: a feedback loop in which minor, demographically asymmetric observation errors compound over time into systemic inequities. We make three contributions. First, we formalize algorithmic drift as a coupled learner--tutor dynamical system. Second, we corroborate this theory with an agent-based Monte Carlo simulation in which either a transparent online tracker or an LSTM deep-knowledge-tracing network drives an IRT-based curriculum policy. Third, we introduce the temporal fairness stabilizer (TFS), a targeted intervention that periodically re-probes learner capability, and benchmark it against standard exploration baselines with explicit group-fairness metrics and replication-level inference. Within the modeled environment, TFS closes the achievement gap where generic exploration does not, and we characterize the conditions under which it succeeds. We separate what is established analytically, what is demonstrated in simulation, and what remains to be validated with real learners. Our results reframe algorithmic fairness in education as a temporal property of a closed loop rather than a deployment-time checkbox.

Multichoice Modeling: Exploring Decision Configuration Space under Uncertainty

Decision support has become sophisticated at exploring uncertainty, but much less so at representing the architecture of the decision itself. As a result, the decision space is often narrowed before uncertainty is analyzed, leaving interactions among choices and the effects of alternatives they imply largely invisible. This paper introduces multichoice modeling, a framework for making large decision configuration spaces explicit and computationally explorable under uncertainty. Drawing on the logic of rugged landscapes, we conceptualize such problems as contingent performance landscapes, where interdependent choices generate configuration-specific outcomes whose value changes across uncertain futures. The computational framework combines multidimensional decision and uncertainty spaces in a single simulation. Further, variance-based sensitivity analysis is used for identifying influential choices, and conditional distribution analysis for comparing configuration-specific performance profiles. We demonstrate the framework through three case studies: a manufacturing process upgrade, M&A strategy, and mortgage design. By contrasting conventional uncertainty-only analysis with multichoice modeling, the cases show how opening the decision configuration space can reveal unexpected complementarities, near-best configurations with different risk profiles, and strategies that were not apparent as alternatives at the outset.

CANNBot-CAKE: Collaborative Agentic Kernel Evolution for Ascend NPU

Efficient custom operators are essential for modern deep learning workloads, yet developing high-performance kernels for Ascend NPUs remains slow, expert-intensive, and difficult to automate. Although AI agents now approach expert performance on GPU kernel benchmarks, current models remain less capable of generating AscendC kernels for NPUs, potentially reflecting the relative scarcity of relevant training data. We thus present CAKE, a CANN-native system for deployable custom operators that combines adaptive multi-DSL routing, separate Generation and Evolution modes, and reviewed knowledge reuse through CAKE Book and a Self-Evolving Agent. On 53 CANN-Bench v1.1 operators on Ascend 950PR, CAKE's aggregated results achieve $100\%$ case pass rate on the standard set (1,060/1,060) and $98.28\%$ on the hidden set (4,167/4,240), with an overall mean score of 78.63 out of 100 and an average performance speedup of $1.334\times$ over the baseline. Evolution then pushes correct operators further: EngramGateFusion cuts aggregate device time by $46.6\%$ over 20 rounds and roughly halves the mean roofline gap. These results offer a practical path toward closing Ascend's AI-for-kernel capability gap.

Mostly Reading: What Open-Book Scores Measure in Document-Level LoRA Adapters

Single-document LoRA adapters have been proposed as units of knowledge to be merged and retrieved; yet open-book scoring, with the document in the prompt, can credit a document's own adapter (its specialist) for reading that document as readily as for holding it. Training one adapter for each of 150 Wikipedia articles on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, we find that open-book scores mostly measure a reading behaviour every adapter shares: a random adapter trained on another document recovers 82 % of the specialist's open-book advantage over the base model but only 2 % of its advantage on a cloze probe that asks for the same document's facts without providing the source text, a share we cannot distinguish from zero. Content does reach the weights, since the specialists beat the base model in cloze recall by 0.094, and the geometry of their updates follows the documents they read; yet, in a test of 30 queries, adapters chosen by weight-space proximity or text embedding are not distinguishable from random ones on either probe, although that test cannot exclude an advantage of about four fifths of what a query's own adapter gains over a random one. At 16 documents or more, correctly scaled merges recover 87 % to 89 % of the specialists' open-book advantage but only 12 % to 16 % of their cloze advantage, with intervals that run from below zero to at most 32 %, against 94 % for joint training.We also report four silent errors, each of which produced a clean, wrong result.

Multi-Tool Selection for Robotic Systems: Comparing Semantic Retrieval and LLM

Natural-language interfaces allow users to command robotic systems without interacting directly with mission planners or robotics middleware. Their reliable use depends on robot tool routing, defined here as the selection of the smallest complete subset of predefined executable capabilities required to satisfy a request. This task is particularly difficult for compound instructions, where incomplete selection may omit necessary sensing or monitoring capabilities, while excessive selection may expose irrelevant tools. We compare four routing strategies: three progressively refined ChromaDB-based methods - fixed-threshold retrieval, relative-distance retrieval, and clause-aware aggregated retrieval - and a cloud-hosted 120-billion-parameter language-model baseline. The methods are evaluated on 164 manually annotated prompts organised into three complexity levels, from direct single-tool requests to compound requests requiring at least three tools. Using exact-match accuracy, completeness, precision, a completeness-weighted composite score, and routing latency, CDB-v3 achieves the best macro-average score among the retrieval-only methods, with 0.7822, compared with 0.8869 for the large-model baseline. Although the LLM provides higher routing quality, CDB-v3 exhibits substantially lower latency and can be deployed locally without requiring cloud connectivity. These results identify a quality-latency trade-off between direct large-model selection and clause-aware semantic retrieval for privacy-sensitive, connectivity-constrained, and resource-constrained robotic systems.

Evaluating Artificial Wisdom (AW): A Trajectory-Based Framework for Context-Sensitive Decision Regulation

Capability benchmarks measure what large language models can do, but not whether their recommendations remain appropriate as decision conditions change. This paper proposes a trajectory-based measurement framework and prospective validation protocol for artificial wisdom, defined behaviorally as context-sensitive regulation of judgment. Versioned reference specifications determine admissible responses and transition materiality before evaluation. Seven stage-level criteria assess judgment under current conditions, while two transition components assess material-update adequacy and commitment-preserving stability. Stability requires prior adequacy; switching within registered commitment classes is permitted, and persistent error earns no stability credit. A nine-component profile is primary, with a secondary bottleneck score and separate critical-failure reporting. Three simplified simulations examine precision, bottleneck estimation, and sensitivity to model-family count. A single-criterion simulation met its precision criteria at 24 scenarios per domain. Near a bottleneck tie, the estimated minimum had a downward bias of 0.55 points at that size. A simplified incremental-prediction simulation detected a Brier improvement of 0.027 with probabilities of .508 and .742 using eight and twelve families, respectively; it does not establish power for the operational protocol. An exploratory predecessor-rubric study comprising 71 evaluation records illustrates aggregation sensitivity but does not validate the proposed instrument. The twelve-family target remains provisional pending feasibility assessment and calibration of the complete analysis. Whether the profile predicts independent decision outcomes beyond capability remains an open empirical question.

Mitigating Saliency Collapse: Robust Saliency-Aware Long-Text Image-Text Alignment

Long-text image-text alignment is essential for fine-grained scene understanding, yet existing methods mainly focus on extending context length or improving local alignment. We identify an overlooked failure mode, saliency collapse, where models over-rely on text that features visual prominence while underutilizing non-salient context. Consequently, retrieval performance degrades sharply when salient content is reduced, despite that the non-salient context remains informative. To address this, we propose RoSA, a saliency-aware finetuning framework that decomposes image-text pairs into complementary salient and contextual views, enabling joint learning of prominent and contextual semantics. We propose a modality-independent decomposition strategy to combine object-level visual saliency estimation with LLM-based text decomposition to bridge vision and language. We employ auxiliary objectives to explicitly align these views, ensuring robust representation with both salient and contextual features. RoSA greatly improves long-text retrieval and demonstrates strong robustness to saliency collapse across benchmarks. Moreover, our auxiliary objectives are plug-and-play, bringing consistent gains to existing tuning methods. Code and checkpoints are available at: https://github.com/intelligolabs/RoSA

Agri-SCHOLAR: Simulation-Grounded In-Season Rolling Advisories for Agricultural Crops Using LLM-Bounded Optimization

Existing agricultural advisories to farmers suffer from several limitations: (a) remain largely expert-driven, (b) do not adequately account for in-season weather, and (c) do not scale to individual farms. In this paper, our objective is to close these gaps by developing Agri-SCHOLAR (Simulation-Grounded Crop Harvest Optimization with LLM-Guided In-Season Advisory Rollouts). Agri-SCHOLAR is a rolling, in-season advisory framework that issues, at intervals of every few days or every week, a personalized advisory using the following information: soil and crop properties, observed weather to date, short-range forecast of weather, and a season extrapolation of weather based on historical analogs. The novelty and depth of Agri-SCHOLAR lies in each advisory being produced by a closed-loop system which couples multiple LLM agents with a powerful crop simulator (such as APSIM in our case). The simulator crucially serves as an evaluation oracle. In Agri-SCHOLAR, the LLM agents propose agronomically sound and feasible bounds on actionable parameters and an optimizer searches within those bounds against a multi-objective reward spanning yield, resource utilization, and economic feasibility. We conduct a detailed 11-year analysis of the maize crop in two selected locations of South Asia and show that Agri-SCHOLAR consistently outperforms the region's standard packages of practice and satisfactorily addresses the limitations of existing agricultural advisories.

SecLog-Bench: A Scalable Benchmark for Cybersecurity Log Reasoning with Scaffolded Evidence

Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for cybersecurity, leading to growing interest in LLM-based automated cybersecurity log analysis. However, current datasets (1) are limited in scale; (2) rely mainly on single-source evidence; and (3) have only binary or multi-class attack labels, without dense annotations of the reasoning process. To address these limitations, SecLog-Bench has three key features. First, we build an extensible simulation platform that covers 29 attack scenarios, supports repeated execution and the integration of new scenarios, and enables scalable generation of cybersecurity logs with attack-stage labels. Second, we use multi-source logs, including audit logs, application logs, and network traffic, to jointly analyze cyber attacks. Third, we annotate each sample with the essential context for cybersecurity log reasoning and a structured step-by-step evidence chain. We evaluate eight mainstream LLMs on this benchmark for post-detection attack-progress reasoning. Results show that, when provided with scaffolded evidence, current LLMs exhibit modest capability in attack-progress reasoning, with the best model achieving only 44.44% accuracy and 45.07% Macro-F1. Their reasoning remains unreliable; i.e., the highest Stage Reasoning F1 (SRF1) score is only 54.87%. Moreover, even correct predictions may be accompanied by flawed reasoning, and reasoning quality is often misaligned with prediction accuracy.

Truthful by Omission: Click Thinning for LLM-Native Advertising

Generative AI assistants are becoming an advertising medium, embedding sponsored content in their answers. This setting departs from classical position auctions in two ways: an ad's click-through rate depends on the passage that hosts it, and because users stop at their first click, earlier ads take clicks from later ones. This competition for clicks makes truthfulness delicate: a higher bid can reshape the allocation and put a competitor ahead of the bidder, reducing the bidder's clicks. Existing mechanisms either attain a constant fraction of the optimal welfare (advertisers' total click value) but are not known to be truthful, or are truthful but their welfare guarantee degrades as the number of passages grows. We achieve both even when the answer and its editorial order are fixed before bidding. Assuming each ad's click-through rate when reached is known and unaffected by omitting other ads, we give a computationally efficient mechanism that is truthful in expectation and attains at least half the optimal welfare under free reordering.
  Our key idea, click thinning, omits ads at random, showing earlier ads less often so that each ad's overall click probability no longer depends on the order of the ads. Welfare maximization then reduces to a tractable matching problem, and solving it exactly with VCG-style payments makes truthful bidding optimal in expectation. Characterizing the click probabilities that omission can achieve sharpens the approximation factor from $2$ to $2-(1-δ)^{K-1}$ when at most $K$ ads are shown and each click-through rate is at most $δ$. This factor is the best possible for any order-preserving rule, even one that ignores incentives and computation, so truthfulness and computational efficiency incur no additional worst-case loss.

ColaBench: Evaluating Memory-Guided Knowledge Acquisition in Personal Agents

Personal agents need to draw on long-term interaction history to retrieve and apply external knowledge suited to a user's current request. Even an answer grounded in relevant documents can be unsuitable when it overlooks the user's circumstances. This creates two connected challenges: using historical conditions to identify the knowledge needed for a new request, and applying that knowledge to the user's situation. Existing benchmarks advance memory recall, personalized retrieval, and memory-guided action, while the joint evaluation of these two challenges remains underexplored. We introduce ColaBench, a synthetic benchmark in health and daily living, with 349 tasks across 10 Profiles and a knowledge base of 13,033 documents. Its Evidence Graph connects personal conditions to their disclosures across public interaction sessions, records prior document exposure, and links answer requirements to personal and newly acquired document evidence. We also introduce MemKB, which uses state summaries to inform queries and rule application, and memory-document links to retrieve knowledge associated with the user's history. MemKB achieves 86.25% Accuracy with an average of 4.07 interaction rounds per task. Task-level analysis finds larger gains on tasks integrating information across multiple historical Facts; error analysis further identifies unsupported personal assumptions and rule misapplication as obstacles to using retrieved evidence. ColaBench provides traceable tasks and an evaluation framework for studying how memory guides knowledge acquisition and rule application.

LAST-V2X: Language-Driven Adaptive Scenario Synthesis and Testing for Communication-Induced Failures in V2X Safety Applications

Vehicle-to-everything (V2X) communication enables connected vehicles to make safety-critical decisions, but delayed, missing, or incorrect information can compromise driving safety. Systematically testing such failures is challenging because traffic interactions, communication conditions, and application responses must be jointly configured to produce meaningful scenarios. This paper presents LAST-V2X, a language-driven, adaptive framework for automated scenario synthesis and safety testing of V2X applications. LAST-V2X leverages large language models (LLMs) to translate natural-language test descriptions into executable scenarios grounded in roadway geometry, V2X communication semantics, and vehicle behavior. The framework integrates physical traffic simulation with spatially aware communication modeling and message-level fault injection to evaluate how information degradation affects vehicle decisions. To establish whether an observed safety failure is caused by communication rather than the underlying traffic conditions, LAST-V2X employs matched executions under degraded and ground-truth information. Furthermore, an execution-guided refinement mechanism combines parameter search and LLM self-reflection to identify ineffective test configurations, improve scenario effectiveness, and produce minimal reproducible failure cases. Experiments across diverse CARLA environments and V2X safety applications demonstrate the framework's ability to generate reliable scenarios, distinguish communication-induced failures from unrelated safety violations, and substantially improve failure discovery through adaptive refinement. These results establish LAST-V2X as a systematic approach to language-driven, causally verified, and adaptive safety testing for connected vehicle systems.
