Anticipating Social Consequences with Distributional World Models

Social world models predict how interactions develop after an action, helping agents negotiate, cooperate and mediate. In these situations, one action can lead to several plausible futures, hence predicting only one future can overlook consequential alternatives, while exploring them through repeated simulations increases inference cost. We propose DSWM, a Distributional Social World Model that predicts alternative responses, their probabilities and immediate and final outcome values in one output per candidate action. We train a 4B model on repeated simulations using supervised fine-tuning and GRPO. At inference time, the actor uses these possible consequences to choose an action. Across three seen and two unseen environments, with interaction backbones absent from training collection, DSWM achieves the best average performance on 13 of 15 metrics. Ablations show that retaining multiple futures and their predicted probabilities improves outcomes in all five environments.

One-shot federated unsupervised domain adaptation with Smoothed Knowledge Distillation and teacher refinement

Federated unsupervised domain adaptation (FUDA) addresses the challenge of adapting models to an unlabeled target domain using decentralized source domains while preserving data privacy. However, existing FUDA methods typically require multiple communication rounds, rely on complex aggregation strategies, and often struggle with noisy pseudo labels and inconsistent source knowledge. To address these challenges, we propose SKD-ETR, a novel one-shot FUDA framework that combines Smoothed Knowledge Distillation (SKD) and Exponential moving average-based Teacher Refinement (ETR). SKD trains a student model on the target domain using smoothed soft pseudo labels generated by the ensemble of source models. This reduces overconfidence, mitigates noise, and improves robustness. ETR further refines each source model by interpolating its parameters toward the student via exponential moving average (EMA), thereby transferring target-domain knowledge back to the teachers. This bidirectional refinement enhances pseudo-label quality and student generalization without additional communication overhead. SKD-ETR eliminates complex aggregation by initializing the student model randomly and performing a single-round distillation process. Extensive experiments on OfficeHome, Office-Caltech, and DomainNet demonstrate that SKD-ETR achieves competitive performance while being communication- and computation-efficient, and robust under noisy supervision. The code for this paper is available at https://github.com/A-Abedi/SKD_ETR.

Speculative Acceptance Does Not Decay with Position: A Full-Length Diagnostic on Long Chain-of-Thought with Independent Drafts

The claim that speculative-decoding acceptance rate decays as a chain-of-thought grows longer has become a de facto industry narrative, motivating draft-model co-design and position-aware training. Nearly all direct evidence comes from trained drafters whose offline training length is far shorter than inference, so a purely positional law has never been isolated. We present the first systematic, full-length measurement of acceptance rate as a function of generation position on MATH-500 (all 500 problems), under greedy and sampling decoding, with two independent drafts against DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B: a same-family draft (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B) and a cross-family draft (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct), yielding about 1.78M verification rounds. Using within-problem paired tests, we find that after isolating a greedy-decoding repetition pathology, acceptance rate does not decay with position in any of the four configurations within the supported ranges (greedy 0-8K, sampling 0-12K); the second half of each trajectory is slightly but significantly higher than the first. A controlled temperature sweep shows that the apparent sampling-mode degradation of high-entropy reflection tokens is a visibility artifact of argmax agreement, not a drop in expected acceptance probability. Rejections concentrate with high precision on reflection trigger tokens such as "wait", "however" and "check", an entropy-selective failure that a lightweight three-arm LoRA trajectory-alignment intervention (uniform, reflection-marked, entropy-weighted) fails to improve in mean accepted length. We thus supply the positional baseline that independent draft-target pairs were missing, and localize the residual failure to decision-point entropy rather than position.

Asking Less, Adapting Better: Feedback-Efficient Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-and-Language Navigation

Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires agents to follow instructions and navigate to target locations. However, current VLN agents often suffer severe performance degradation in unseen environments due to distribution shifts. Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) offers a promising paradigm to bridge this gap, yet existing methods that rely on unsupervised entropy minimization can propagate errors through the trajectory, causing cascading failures in navigation. Recent methods mitigate this by incorporating human feedback, but they apply trajectory-level supervision uniformly across all steps without localizing the actions is truly informative, leading to inefficient feedback utilization and mismatched optimization granularity. To address these limitations, we propose FETA, a Feedback-Efficient Test-Time Adaptation method that improves both feedback efficiency and optimization reliability. To reduce unnecessary human interventions, we introduce a hierarchical sample selection strategy to localize informative samples. Specifically, we first filter trajectories whose normalized policy entropy exhibits large fluctuations. Within each selected trajectory, we further select the critical action via an entropy-gradient saliency criterion and query a single binary feedback on its correctness. A discrepancy-aware entropy optimization objective then updates the policy only at the queried step, with adaptive scaling that assigns larger relative weights to samples with greater prediction-feedback discrepancy. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that FETA achieves state-of-the-art performance with fewer human interventions, while remaining robust under noisy feedback and continual adaptation settings.

Large language models show systematic failures in incorporating evolving medical knowledge

Medical knowledge evolves as clinical guidelines revise recommendations, yet whether large language models (LLMs) faithfully incorporate such changes remains unclear. We introduce ChronoEvo, comprising 1,709 recommendation-level revisions across 12 forms of medical knowledge change, to distinguish recognition of revised guidance from incorporation into clinical recommendations. In the primary model, 90.8% of responses recognized revisions and 80.2% were clinically correct, but only 58.5% fully incorporated guidance, yielding a 32.3-percentage-point recognition-incorporation gap. This dissociation persisted across 14 models and was corroborated by two clinicians' adjudicated assessment of 300 responses, yielding a 40.3-percentage-point gap in the validation sample. Changes in recommendation strength and patient eligibility were associated with retention of outdated guidance. A revision-salience intervention reduced outdated-content retention and improved clinical correctness but did not significantly improve full incorporation. These findings identify faithful incorporation of evolving medical knowledge as a distinct challenge for clinical LLM reliability, even when updated guideline evidence is available.

Interpreting Model Reasoning through Behavioral Counterfactuals

Faithfully understanding the internal process that occurs as models form their chain of thought is an important problem with the rise in model capabilities. However, this remains challenging due to the variance in sampled responses, making it difficult to isolate the effects of interventions and compare model reasoning systematically. We propose a method for localizing and analyzing how a given intervention affects the model's reasoning through behavioral counterfactuals: given a fixed response, how does introducing an intervention to the prompt change the next-token distributions? We train a module to edit the residual stream such that it minimizes the KL divergence between the output distributions for a fixed response under a source prompt and its counterfactual target prompt. We find that matching the output distributions recovers the counterfactual behavior more effectively than standard activation reconstruction objectives and validate that attribution of the trained module outputs is effective for identifying causally important locations for counterfactual recovery. The attribution maps and visualizations of the downstream effects of model edits provide an interface for generating hypotheses about the original model's computation, which can then be tested with activation patching. In a case study on Gemma-4-E4B-it and DeepSeek-R1-0528-Qwen3-8B, we investigate how a suggestion influences model reasoning even when it is not verbally acknowledged. Our analysis identifies representations at the question statement and filler tokens that mediate a substantial part of the suggestion's influence, with downstream effects such as changing the framing of the justification or even the final answer.

Future of Time Series Anomaly Detection: From Historical Review to Unified Outlook

Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) is a foundational problem in data mining, aiming to identify observations that deviate significantly from nominal temporal patterns. Driven by escalating data volumes and application complexity, recent research has yielded diverse architectures, including diffusion models, state space models, and large language models (LLMs). However, existing surveys provide limited coverage of these emerging designs and lack a unified treatment of multivariate anomalies, cross-domain unified modeling, metric reliability, and contemporary evaluation challenges. To bridge these gaps, this survey delivers three core contributions: (1) We establish a refined anomaly categorization and a structured taxonomy of paradigms reflecting modern advances, comparing detection mechanisms and charting developmental trajectories across paradigms. (2) We provide an extensive analysis of evaluation methodologies and conduct comprehensive experiments to assess metric fidelity, complemented by an open-source benchmark repository encompassing algorithms, datasets, and evaluation tools. (3) We outline key open challenges, offering forward-looking perspectives on dynamic thresholding, model interpretability, zero-shot transfer, multi-modal alignment, and foundational models for time series. By addressing existing theoretical and empirical limitations, this survey establishes a coherent framework to guide future TSAD research and practical deployments.

Better Reasoning, Not Just Bigger Models: A Structure-Grounded Framework for Efficient and Reliable Inference

Scaling large language models has substantially improved linguistic fluency, yet reliable autonomous agents require more than fluent generation. In multi-turn inference, current agentic systems often produce semantically plausible but structurally ungrounded reasoning, especially under incomplete observations. We propose Structure-Grounded Uncertainty Reduction (SGUR), a training-free framework that complements token-level generation with active uncertainty reduction over structured hypothesis spaces. SGUR represents incomplete observations as a query graph and aligns them with candidate hypothesis graphs through a prior-weighted partial graph matching mechanism, where database-derived empirical priors assign greater importance to decision-relevant relations rather than treating all graph edges uniformly. The resulting inference state is iteratively refined by an active information-gain strategy that selects the next most informative node to query, systematically pruning the candidate space. We instantiate SGUR on a challenging PrimeKG-derived sequential medical diagnosis benchmark, demonstrating that structural grounding can partially substitute for model scale: starting from only two observed symptoms, SGUR achieves 92.5% Hit@3, outperforming the strongest Graph-RAG baseline by 9.4 percentage points while using approximately 5x fewer tokens. Notably, SGUR enables smaller backbone models to match or exceed the performance of models up to 10x larger, suggesting that structural grounding provides a more robust and efficient paradigm for scaling agent performance than relying exclusively on increasing model size.

Impacts of generative AI skill development on academic adaptation and learning outcomes among university students

Understanding how university students perceive the development of their generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) skills over time can clarify how GenAI use relates to learning and academic outcomes. This study examined perceived skill-development trajectories, changes in learning practices, and associated academic gains using a retrospective questionnaire with 313 analyzed responses and interviews with 18 students across subject areas. Descriptive and comparative analyses and exploratory structural equation modeling (SEM) were integrated with thematic analysis. Perceived developmental trajectories were heterogeneous: 34.2% of students reported rapid early improvement followed by a plateau, 28.4% steady improvement, and 21.1% delayed acceleration. Development was also uneven across skills, with improvement in evaluating GenAI outputs perceived as more challenging; assignments were the most frequently selected driver of skill growth. Interviews further indicated increasingly specific prompting, tailored verification, and selective delegation, although some students reported stable routines, dependence, skipped reasoning, or improvements attributable partly to better GenAI tools. Academic gains were reported more often for assignments than for examinations. The SEM indicated a positive indirect association between perceived skill growth and academic gains through adaptation of assignment processes, whereas academic gains did not clearly differ across developmental trajectory groups after multiplicity correction. The findings suggest that perceived GenAI skill development varies in both trajectory and skill domain and is associated with changes in learning practices and differentiated academic benefits.

Visual Grounding First, Multimodal In-context Learning Follows

LLMs have shown strong in-context learning (ICL) capability, but extending it to Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) remains challenging. Prior multimodal ICL methods often rely on ICL-specific datasets for additional training or task vector extraction, which can improve performance on the same ICL benchmark used for adaptation. However, they do not generalize well to other benchmarks and often induce forgetting in previously well-solved tasks. For this reason, we identify 'visual grounding' as the key bottleneck in multimodal ICL; MLLMs often fail to attend to task-relevant visual evidence in demonstrations, instead over-relying on textual cues and producing hallucinated outputs. To address this, we propose ProCoRe to enhance visual grounding through contrastive reinforcement learning on multimodal contrastive data for improving multimodal ICL. ProCoRe generates captions for contrastive image pairs, decomposes them into propositions, and optimizes contrastive rewards that encourage alignment with corresponding images while discouraging mismatched ones. Despite never seeing ICL-formatted training examples, ProCoRe improves average multimodal ICL classification accuracy by 5.6% and captioning ROUGE-L by 13.8% over the off-the-shelf Qwen-VL-3-8B model, outperforming ICL-data-dependent state of the arts across benchmarks. Finally, we introduce PICL, a personalized multimodal ICL captioning benchmark that tests whether MLLMs genuinely understand visual demonstrations rather than relying on pre-trained semantic priors.

Model Organisms of Collusion: Corrective Transfer in Untrusted Monitors

AI control protocols aim to get useful work from models that may be misaligned by monitoring their behaviour. One approach uses a second copy of the same model as the monitor. This avoids relying on a weaker reviewer the model could outsmart, but risks collusion: the model, now monitoring itself, may cover up its own misbehaviour. One remedy is to fine-tune the monitor on whatever examples of misbehaviour the control team has caught. We ask whether this training also removes collusion that the control team has not yet found. We fine-tune Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B to install five policy-monitor behaviour pairs, correct the monitor on 300 honest reviews of one caught collusion, and measure collusion on the other four in unseen prompt formats. Averaged over the three seeds of each family, correction reduces held-out collusion from 91% to 14-20% under three of the four methods we compare (individual seeds 10-32%), while a control fine-tuned on the same number of unrelated examples leaves 56-93%. Family-average accuracy on control items changes by at most five points and capability is preserved, apart from a drop in mathematical accuracy on one Llama seed. Under the tested training configuration, methods with similar caught-task accuracy differ in held-out transfer, from 58% to 83% average reduction, so the caught behaviour alone does not reveal how far a correction reaches. Catching one collusion lets the control team reduce, but not remove, collusion it has not caught, by retraining the monitor alone.

Bridging the Closed-World Gap: Interpretable Anomaly Detection via a Hybrid TSK-FCM Ensemble on the BETH Dataset

A fundamental paradox plagues the deployment of Machine Learning in cybersecurity: supervised models generalize poorly to novel, Out-of-Distribution (OoD) attacks, whereas unsupervised models often suffer from poor separability, leading to unmanageable false-alarm rates. This challenge is exemplified by the BETH dataset, a kernel-level benchmark in which the training data consists solely of benign and suspicious insider behaviors, whereas the test set consists almost entirely of novel botnet activation attacks. To bridge this detection gap, we propose a Hybrid Fuzzy Ensemble that synergizes the precision of supervised learning with the adaptability of unsupervised clustering. Our architecture integrates a gradient-optimized Takagi-Sugeno-Kang (TSK) fuzzy classifier to act as an expert on known insider threats, with a parallel Fuzzy C-Means (FCM) module to detect geometric anomalies. A critical finding of our research is that traditional fuzzy membership scores degrade in high-dimensional feature spaces due to the curse of dimensionality. We overcome this by introducing a robust Min Distance metric that exploits the raw geometric isolation of botnet activation attacks. This approach yields a state-of-the-art AUROC of 0.9951 on the BETH test set, outperforming the original Isolation Forest baseline by 17.1%. By combining near-perfect OoD detection with the intrinsic interpretability of fuzzy logic, our framework offers a viable path toward robust, transparent, and autonomous network defense.

Diagnosing Personalization Failures in Mobile GUI Agents

Unlike conventional GUI agents, personalized GUI agents require user preferences to guide sequential execution over changing GUI states. This raises two challenges: determining HOW each preference should alter execution strategy and WHEN it should take effect along the interaction trajectory. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate overall task success, offering limited insight into these questions. We introduce PrefDiag-Bench with two components: (1) a categorization of preferences into five types, characterizing HOW they affect execution; and (2) a stage-wise diagnostic framework with 1,664 probes, tracing WHEN preferences should take effect and where failures occur. We evaluate eleven GUI agent systems, including end-to-end agents and agentic workflows, under two forms of user context: explicit user profiles and behavioral memory logs. Although existing agents achieve relatively high completion rates on non-personalized tasks, performance is substantially lower on preference-personalized tasks. Even the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves only 47.17% on Process preferences under explicit user profiles. Agents struggle particularly with Ranking and Process preferences. Further analysis reveals two key capability bottlenecks: selecting relevant evidence from user history and recognizing when preferences should take effect during execution. Controlled interventions that explicitly provide relevant evidence improve end-to-end task success by up to 7.06%. PrefDiag-Bench offers a more systematic evaluation of GUI agents' personalization capabilities beyond overall task success. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Zhixin-L/PrefDiag-Bench.

Lifetime-valid decisions by continually evolving AI analysts

AI agents increasingly act as analysts whose decision can often be cast as hypothesis tests. Classical tests control the Type I error rate, the probability of rejecting a true null hypothesis, for fixed procedures. However, AI analysts introduce internal randomness and can evolve continuously over time. As AI analysts evolve, the risk of approving versions with Type I error rates above the target can accumulate, and adaptive use of evaluation feedback can lead to overfitting. We introduce continual validation, which evaluates each proposed AI analyst version before deployment using simulated data under the null hypothesis, with scenarios that can be randomized and expanded over time. We prove that, regardless of how updates are chosen or how many are proposed, the lifetime probability of ever deploying a version whose Type I error rate exceeds the allowed level remains bounded. This guarantees that every subsequent decision controls the Type I error rate at the target level across these scenarios. At the same time, updates with sufficiently low Type I error rates pass validation with high probability, allowing the analyst to continuously improve its performance without sacrificing rigor. In two examples involving evolving AI analysts for bioequivalence studies and product A/B tests, Type I error rates reached 0.098 and 0.183 without continual validation, compared with at most 0.045 and 0.044 with it, while retaining 82% and 96% of the power from optimally selected valid updates.

Governed Self-Evolution for Autonomous Investing: A Multi-Agent LLM System with an Invariant Core

LLM agents that act on live Web data must combine heterogeneous evidence, remember what they have done and improve from outcomes without weakening their own safeguards. We study this problem in autonomous investing, where every action is irreversible and based on untrusted Web content. We present Steward, a multi-agent LLM system that draws the boundary of self-evolution in its type system. First, nine role-specialised agents decide jointly: four analysts read news feeds, SEC filings, macroeconomic series and market data and emit typed reports, from which a Portfolio Manager sets target positions that a protected Risk Manager audits. Second, a seven-layer shared memory, assembled by code from the system's own record, gives each decision its position context, the market narrative, calibration and the agent's past decisions. Third, the system improves itself by review: code grades every trade and forecast nightly and writes the grades into memory, and each quarter a reflector proposes prompt learnings that a deterministic gate applies reversibly and replay of recorded inputs verifies. All three operate around an invariant core of order arithmetic, risk rules and discipline-agent prompts that no self-edit can reach. In a five-month live deployment (102 trading days, 1,632 LLM calls), the system contained a 24.6% sector drawdown to a 0.3% loss; in replay of its recorded inputs, a single model given the same raw data traded more and lost 1.35 points against the market over five days where the joint design showed no significant loss, and no injected instruction was followed; and a 2015-2026 backtest shows the core alone lowering the maximum drawdown under all 50 random entry policies. The reflector traced the dominant failure to its root cause, and the gate applied a learning that the agent adopted on trigger evenings and on no control evening. Code: https://github.com/yebof/quant-agent

PLE: Physical-Engineering's Last Exam

Frontier language models now score above 90% on popular knowledge benchmarks and are increasingly deployed as agents that carry out multi-hour professional work. Yet no existing benchmark tests whether they can perform the real-world, economically valuable work of a professional physical engineer, such as modelling physics and legal code requirements across a multi-format project corpus, optimising material and installation costs, and issuing 2D/3D engineering design packages that a licensed reviewer would sign off on. Existing engineering benchmarks focus on textbook question answering, isolated tool calls or self-contained toy design problems. As a result, most of these benchmarks are already saturated by leading agents and solving them is far away from delivering real engineering design. We introduce PLE, a benchmark of 85 long-horizon, agentic tasks in professional physical engineering consisting of electrical, civil, structural and mechanical engineering, where solving a task means completing a realistic, billable engineering deliverable. PLE combines two complementary curation paths. Expert-Authored tasks are created end to end by practising engineers from a professional data provider's expert pool. Co-Authored tasks start from problem statements written by practising engineers; an adversarial Creator-Solver agent pipeline constructs each task's realistic project environment, executable grader and reference deliverables before sending them to expert engineers for validation and difficulty intensification. We evaluate a variety of frontier LLMs under seven publicly available agent harnesses. Even the strongest configuration reaches a mean score of only 50.6%, while spending 27M tokens and 63 minutes per task on average, demonstrating the substantial gap between current agents and autonomous physical engineering design.

Decision Making and Ride Comfort Evaluation Based on Lane-Change Prediction for Autonomous Vehicles

Predicting surrounding vehicles' lane-change intentions enables autonomous vehicles to anticipate potential hazards and respond proactively rather than relying solely on reactive maneuvers. This study investigates the benefits of integrating lane-change prediction, based on knowledge graph embeddings and Bayesian inference, into autonomous vehicle decision-making. Three driving strategies are evaluated in risky merging scenarios simulated in CARLA: reactive driving, prediction-based longitudinal action, and prediction-based lateral action. The proposed prediction model anticipates the surrounding vehicle's lane change approximately 3-4 seconds before its occurrence, allowing the ego vehicle to adjust its behavior accordingly. In the representative simulation records, reactive driving results in a collision accompanied by aggressive deceleration. In contrast, prediction-based longitudinal action initiates early braking to establish a safe gap, while prediction-based lateral action performs a lane change when a safe adjacent lane is available. Both predictive strategies avoid collisions in the evaluated records. Compared with reactive driving, peak deceleration before impact is reduced by 31.5% with longitudinal action and 91.0% with lateral action. Additionally, an ISO 2631-1-based horizontal vibration estimate is used to provide a ride comfort assessment. Overall, the results highlight the potential of lane-change prediction to support safer and smoother driving through proactive decision-making, with additional insights into ride comfort provided by vibration-based estimates.

LLM2Cert: LLM Synthesis of Certificates with SMT/Lean Verification

Lyapunov functions and barrier certificates give state-space guarantees of stability and safety, but synthesizing and verifying them---especially for nonlinear, high-dimensional systems---remains an open challenge. Existing pipelines verify candidates with numerical solvers (sum-of-squares programs), interval methods, or satisfiability-modulo-theories (SMT) solvers, which often require relaxations, are conservative, or scale poorly with model dimension and nonlinearity. These limitations have also shaped benchmark design: standard test suites are dominated by low-dimensional polynomial systems chosen to suit what solvers can handle rather than what engineering applications require. We address both sides of this challenge. First, we introduce DynaCertBench, a benchmark of 105 instances: 80 non-redundant systems curated from the literature, extended with high-dimensional and control-constrained variants and new nonlinearly coupled systems, comprehensively spanning stability, region-of-attraction, forward-invariance, and safety tasks. Second, we present LLM2Cert, an LLM-driven framework that synthesizes candidate certificates and verifies them against their proof obligations: each obligation is routed to an SMT solver or directly to the Lean Proof-Repair agent. On DynaCertBench, LLM2Cert verifies 81 of 105 instances ($77.1\%$), a $17.1$ percentage point improvement over one-shot proposal without feedback. On the 81 fixed certificates, LLM2Cert verifies 22 that All-SMT leaves unresolved. Code and data are available at https://github.com/FormalCert-AI/llm2cert.

Protegrity-PII Technical Report

We present Protegrity-PII, a new PII decision model for structured and unstructured text. Protegrity-PII achieves a typed recall of 0.915 with a precision of 0.907 on a new benchmark we propose, PII-SCOPE. Due in part to the expansive nature of the entity type taxonomy used in training, Protegrity-PII achieves superior out-of-distribution generalization when benchmarked against a selection of state of the art models and datasets.

Behind Aggregate Scores: What Systematically Varying Arena-Hard's LLM Judge Reveals

LLM judges are used to determine which of two answers is better. Trusting these verdicts requires knowing how repeatable they are and how much they depend on the judge's instructions. We show that similar aggregate scores can conceal substantial changes in individual verdicts, both when the same setup is repeated and when the instructions change. Using 500 Arena-Hard v2.0 prompts with two models' answers held fixed, we run GPT-4.1 as the judge under all eight combinations of criteria, output format and task framing, and repeat every setup unchanged at temperature zero on a pinned model version. Rerunning the default setup moves the aggregate score by about one point but changes the win/tie/loss outcome on 21.2% of prompts. Instruction changes introduce further sensitivity: in one setup, which asks for the verdict without reasoning and also drops several other instructions, outcomes change on 28.2 percentage points more prompts than the two setups' unchanged reruns do on average, and the aggregate score falls by 4.09 points. In another, where the criteria prioritise accuracy, outcomes change on 6.7 percentage points more prompts by the same comparison, while the aggregate score barely moves. A stable aggregate score therefore does not establish that individual verdicts are repeatable. This distinction matters when those verdicts are used as preference labels or to assess individual responses. Evaluations that rely on individual verdicts should repeat each setup they use and report both aggregate scores and prompt-level agreement.

PhysibleMDM: Enforcing Inference-Time Physical Feasibility Constraints on Motion Diffusion Models via Noise Optimization

Motion Diffusion Models (MDMs) can generate diverse and visually appealing human motion; yet, the resulting trajectories frequently violate physical constraints, limiting their downstream use in simulation and control. We present PhysibleMDM, a framework that promotes physical feasibility of MDM output by optimizing at inference time the diffusion noise without retraining the generative model. We introduce a set of surrogate feasibility constraints that can be evaluated without full trajectory simulation. These constraints combine kinematic checks (e.g., ground penetration, slip, and self-collision) with dynamic feasibility tests computed via an inverse-dynamics inner loop formulated as a cone program. We solve the resulting black-box noise optimization problem using the Separable Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (Sep-CMA-ES), thereby avoiding reliance on computationally expensive and discontinuous gradients. An autoregressive scheme then chains optimized segments to synthesize long motion sequences. Across 100 motion styles on an SMPL humanoid, PhysibleMDM reduces mean ground penetration, self-collision penetration, and ground slip by 75%, 92%, and 74%, respectively, relative to unoptimized diffusion output. Across 11 sequences for a custom bipedal robot, it achieves median reductions of 89.5% in per-sequence mean acceleration-reconstruction error and 79.5% in the mean inverse-dynamics residual.

Can LLMs Improve Their Own Harness? A Controlled Study on ARC-AGI-3

A generally intelligent system acquires new skills efficiently without requiring humans to redesign its software scaffold for every new task. Yet language models rarely act alone. A harness is a symbolic program that orchestrates calls to the model, handling perception, memory, tool use, recovery, and inference allocation on the model's behalf. ARC-AGI-3 is an interactive benchmark in which agents must explore unfamiliar environments, figure out their goals on the fly, and keep learning as they go. On it, human-steered systems already report ceiling scores across the public games, so we know effective designs exist. We ask whether frontier models can build and improve such a harness on their own. Through a fixed coding bootstrap, each model constructs a complete task harness, acts through that harness on five development games, and revises the entire codebase twice. Every version is evaluated on twenty games that never influence adaptation. We measure task performance with Relative Human Action Efficiency (RHAE), which combines level completion with action efficiency relative to human baselines, and record dollar-denominated inference cost as a practical resource measure. Across 27 lineages, harness performance ranges from zero to near-ceiling scores. Two revisions raise mean held-out RHAE by only 2.80 points (95% interval [-1.44,7.35]). Development gains overstate held-out improvement, and reductions in inference cost can come at the expense of task performance. Human-steered systems demonstrate further headroom. These findings reveal a gap between autonomous harness construction and reliable adaptation under this two-revision protocol: even strong task performance does not ensure that subsequent revisions generalize or preserve existing capabilities.

From Prose to Properties: Testing Research Claims with ARATest for Agent-Native Science

AI agents now run experiments and report findings, so research produces claims faster than researchers can check them. Each empirical claim implies a property that must hold over its cases, but it states that property only in prose. Without an explicit property, no test can decide which evidence would falsify a claim, so validation cannot keep pace with the claims agents produce. To make claims testable, we approximate a claim's semantics by abstracting its property skeleton, a parameterized relation that recurs across claims. We present ARATest, a property-based testing library that checks these skeletons as executable tests. A typed declaration binds a claim to a skeleton and fixes the claim's compared quantities, cases, scope, and numerical policy. A shared case type makes every generator supply cases in the claim's statistical unit. A three-valued semantics assigns each property one of three verdicts: holds, violated, or inconclusive. Every violation carries a replayable witness. A default generator reads the evidence that agent-native research artifacts (ARA) link to each claim, and auto-research generators dispatch an agent to run released code for fresh cases. Our DissClaimer plugin supplies that agent and runs these generators as continuous testing.
  Of 243 claims in 33 ARA artifacts, 54 received faithful declarations bound to evidence: 32 held, eight were violated, and 14 remained inconclusive. Two violations exposed errors in published papers that their authors confirmed. ARATest detected all 553 seeded faults without a false alarm on 19 valid controls, outperforming two LLM judges. Fresh runs exposed behaviors that reported evidence could not show. These results suggest that one fixed test can check explicit claim properties against both reported and fresh evidence.

What the Trace Shows and What the Label Says: Auditing Behavioral Detectors in Agent Evaluation

LLM agents are often evaluated by reducing execution traces to labels such as final-answer correctness, tool verification, and fault propagation. These labels are measurements produced by parsers, behavioral detectors, and harness rules, and they may not fully capture what the trace shows. We study this in a tool-use setting built on GSM8K, where agents call a calculator whose outputs are occasionally corrupted, giving clear ground truth for what the tool returned, what the model later used, and what the detectors reported. We audit three trace-level detectors across six production models and 30 problems per model. Final correctness, visible re-verification, and fault propagation can disagree in systematic ways. A key failure mode is silent tool-output override: the calculator returns a corrupted value, but the model later uses a different, often correct, value without calling the tool again, so the bad value stops propagating while a verification detector records no re-check. Human review of 90 traces agrees perfectly with the automated labels for final correctness (67/67), fault impact (19/19), and fault relevance (18/18), but only 67/81 for verification; all 14 disagreements fall into four recurring patterns. Execution limits also change the reported result: in an ablation across two models, one ranges from 21/30 to 27/30 correct as step and token budgets change, while the other stays between 27/30 and 29/30. These findings motivate measurement transparency: an automated label should come with the trace evidence behind it, whether the question the detector asks applied to that trace, whether the run was cut off by an execution limit, and any human corrections. Our trace viewer makes this information inspectable alongside the reported results.

From Single-Actor Environments to Multi-Actor Event Worlds: Can LLMs Be Event World Models?

World models help agents assess the consequences of their actions before acting. Many real systems, however, involve multiple actors who make their own decisions, can evolve even when the agent takes no action, and are only partially observed through event logs. We formalize these systems as event worlds and ask whether large language models can predict future observations from the log and the agent's proposed actions. We build complementary benchmarks from human Diplomacy games and real Polymarket trades. Our hierarchical framework compares prediction with and without other actors' actions, then tests multi-step rollouts, predictions for actions never taken, and usefulness to agents. Fine-tuning and more data improve prediction when all actors' recorded actions are given. With only the agent's action, models struggle when other actors strongly affect the outcome, and larger models and longer histories help little. This gap also limits multi-step prediction and agent decisions, with models overestimating plan success. Yet imagined experience and initialization from world models can still improve policy learning. Anticipating other actors' actions remains a central challenge for event world modeling.

Why Bother Me Again? Benchmarking Personal Agent Memory for Interaction Preferences

Personal intelligence systems are taking on increasingly complex everyday tasks, yet repeated questions, unnecessary confirmations, and unwanted outreach can turn capable assistance into interaction burden. Reducing this burden requires agents to remember how users want to be assisted. However, existing memory benchmarks focus on factual recall and personalized answers and rarely test how agents learn and act on interaction preferences. We introduce MemPABench, an interactive environment for on-policy evaluation of memory in personal agents. MemPABench poses long-horizon tasks requiring agents to decide when to clarify, confirm, or act autonomously. Four grounded simulated users hold 14 preferences across work and personal contexts. Their feedback spans 99 sessions over four months, with five event-driven changes per user testing memory updates. We evaluate preference selection, delivered behavior, and sensitivity to context and preference change. Evaluating four memory systems across four LLM backbones, we find that current memory brings limited gains in preference selection. Retrieval-based systems (i.e. Mem0, MemOS, simple RAG) do not outperform No Memory, while File-based memory improves accuracy but applies preferences beyond relevant contexts. Our analysis shows that current memory struggles to retrieve interaction preferences and adapt to preference changes. Even with the correct preference available, overriding the agent's default interaction habits remains challenging. The environment and dataset are available at https://mempabench.com.

Externalise the Memory: A Pre-Registered Study of Where an Embodied Agent's Experience Should Live

Should an embodied agent's experience be baked into its policy weights, or kept external and retrieved at inference? A memory-augmented teacher (a VLM that retrieves lessons, with their episode images, from its own past attempts) produces trajectories on a vision-conditioned reach task; from the same trajectories we distil LoRA student policies and evaluate them under matched protocols, including one that re-attaches the teacher's frozen retrieval buffer to a fixed set of student weights. We pre-registered the hypotheses, arms, tests, and analysis rules before training and before any p-value. Distilling the memory-augmented behaviour into weights beat an action-only control by +1 pp (n.s.); the registered test proves underpowered for its own minimum effect, so we withdraw - rather than refute - the memory-in-weights claim. Attaching retrieval to a fixed set of student weights added +34 pp (z=5.15), reaching the pooled memory-augmented teacher reference (49.3%); the pre-registered protocol contrast agrees (+23 pp, z=3.36); under a pre-registered auto-reset sensitivity count the two are +36 and +29 pp. An exploratory behavioural probe with pre-registered per-arm predictions is consistent with behavioural distillation transferring the marginal correction but not the conditional, situation-specific correction that retrieval supplies. The recipe that follows is to externalise the memory: in follow-up controls registered before they ran, the same frozen buffer lifts the base model with no adapter to 48% (sensitivity 48%) and the other three adapters from 19-26% to 53-55% (sensitivity 17-21% to 50-55%), and the adapter adds nothing detectable once the buffer is attached. On a second, harder task, the retrieval advantage points the same direction but falls short of significance (+6 pp, 95% CI [-4.5, +16.4]); exploratory diagnostics tie the attenuation to memory coverage.

Rethinking SAE Evaluation: An Atomic Interpretable Unit Framework Reveals Hidden Polysemanticity and Redundancy

Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) were originally motivated by discovering monosemantic features, a property that characterizes individual features but does not specify what the learned feature set should look like as a whole. As SAEs have increasingly been adopted for attribution, circuit discovery, and intervention, researchers have begun evaluating metrics that describe global properties of the feature set, such as sparsity, dead feature rates, reconstruction fidelity, and disentanglement, a diverse set that extends far beyond monosemanticity. However, this implicit shift beyond monosemanticity has not been formalized as a clear, well-defined objective that comes before these evaluation metrics. Without such an objective, existing metrics remain ad hoc, with their necessity unjustified. In this work, we formalize this ultimate objective by introducing a new paradigm for SAE evaluation: requiring that SAE features, and more generally dictionary-based decompositions of model representations, form an atomic interpretable unit basis, a coordinate system for semantically meaningful explanation. Under this paradigm, we organize existing and new measurements such as DAME (Dominance-Aware Monosemanticity Evaluation) as proxy metrics and introduce a comprehensive benchmarking framework. Evaluating hundreds of SAEs across multiple architectures and model families, we uncover failure modes in which some SAEs appear monosemantic under existing evaluation metrics while remaining functionally polysemantic: the most dominant features, those contributing most to model computation, are systematically less interpretable and more redundant. These failure modes warrant attention from SAE researchers as targets for improved training and architectures, and motivate dominance-aware evaluation as a standard check for practitioners before SAE features are deployed for attribution, circuit tracing, or intervention.

SplatLab: Autonomous Scientific Discovery for 3DGS Research

Reproducing a published 3DGS paper takes weeks of expert engineering before any extension can be proposed. Prior autonomous-research systems read papers, draft hypotheses, and write code, but are limited by short fixed templates, low executability, and weak traceability, and none has been demonstrated end-to-end in a live computer-vision subfield with intense activity. SplatLab is an autonomous research system for 3D Gaussian Splatting that addresses these limitations. Given a research prompt, a target-paper URL, or no input at all, it reproduces a published baseline, proposes an extension, trains and ablates across Mip-NeRF 360 and LLFF, writes the manuscript, and binds every reported number to a logged training run. Five design choices distinguish it: (i) candidates mutate plugins from a verified-reproduction backbone matched to published baselines; (ii) a cascade-weighted Elo tournament across eight islands scores candidates on measured GPU PSNR with verbal-gradient feedback; (iii) a standing falsifier subtracts Elo on failure, integrating stress-test survival into selection; (iv) every reported number is bound to a logged training run, and the run cannot terminate while any claim is unbacked; (v) a seven-category 3DGS taxonomy wraps EVOLVE-BLOCK markers in domain-aligned scaffolds transferable across 3DGS repositories. On PSNR-targeted runs, SplatLab's manuscripts beat their reproduced targets by +0.18 to +3.78 dB, including +3.78 dB on astrophotographic nebula rendering, for which no prior 3DGS method exists. The research cycle compresses from months of graduate-student time to days of mostly autonomous compute. Code, all manuscripts, experiment ledgers, and the cross-run paper store are available on the project page.

Short-to-Long: Detached Features for Long-Horizon Value Learning

Learning useful representations from continually changing value targets remains a challenge in deep reinforcement learning. We study the temporal horizon of this supervision: smaller discounts make these targets less sensitive to distant bootstrap changes, but using them for behavior changes the control objective. We introduce Short-to-Long (S2L), which supplies a long-horizon behavior learner with features trained by smaller-discount source networks. Detached connections prevent the behavior value loss from updating the sources, allowing each source to follow its own value objective while the behavior discount remains unchanged. Across Atari, online continuous control, and offline continuous control, the approach yields aggregate performance gains, improving the paired terminal median on 40 of 57 Atari games. Matched Atari ablations support the roles of feature transfer, smaller source discounts, and gradient isolation. Fixed-trajectory measurements further show reduced drift in source value targets with mixed discounts.

aggmetrics: Aggregating Classification Performance Measures for Machine Learning

aggmetrics is a Python package that extends any binary performance measure expressed in the four confusion-matrix counts to multiclass, multilabel, ordinal, cost-sensitive, soft-label and cross-validation settings, by substituting one of four aggregation operators for what libraries usually expose as an average switch. The package also stores, for every measure, the structural properties that decide which identities apply to it, can infer those properties from a user-supplied formula, and uses them to warn when a measure is used where it does not behave as commonly assumed. Its test suite encodes the results of the companion theory as executable tests. The package is BSD-3 licensed, depends only on NumPy, and is available at https://github.com/rcprati/aggmetrics; documentation at https://aggmetrics.readthedocs.io.

Same Beliefs, Different Policies: Learning Reusable Representations for Early Stopping in LLM Reasoning

Reasoning LLMs often continue reasoning after reaching a correct answer, wasting computation and sometimes replacing it with an incorrect one. Deciding when to stop is a metareasoning problem: the model must weigh what further reasoning could achieve against its cost, given what it can already answer. Direct utility training learns a stopping representation at a chosen token price; supporting new prices can require additional controller training. We introduce BELT (BELief-based Termination), which separates outcome prediction from stopping control. It learns beliefs about current and future answers from a frozen LLM's hidden states, then fits a linear stopping policy on the frozen features. The prediction targets are independent of token price, so one probe supports different accuracy-cost trade-offs through CPU policy refitting or threshold adjustment. Averaged over five out-of-distribution (OOD) math benchmarks, BELT reduces generated tokens by 41% on Qwen3-1.7B and 51% on OLMo-3-7B-Think without loss of accuracy. The same math-trained BELT transfers to coding and agentic tasks without training on code or agentic data. In all three domains, its accuracy-cost trade-offs are competitive with or better than those of end-to-end controllers. On OLMo3, its probe has 21.2x fewer parameters than one checkpoint of the strongest baseline, OS-Pruner, and needs 55x fewer training GPU-hours than OS-Pruner's five price-specific checkpoints.

A Three-Pillar Governance Framework for Safe and Trustworthy AI Agents

AI agents capable of autonomous decision-making introduce significant challenges related to safety, alignment, accountability, and human oversight. Existing Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) and responsible AI approaches provide important foundations, but many remain fragmented across system design, governance, and operational deployment. This paper presents a Three-Pillar (3P) model and framework for developing safe and trustworthy AI agents through transparency, accountability, and trustworthiness. The framework defines an evolutionary path in which increasing agent autonomy is introduced through progressive validation, structured human oversight, and auditable operational controls. We further describe how the model supports agent lifecycle monitoring, decision traceability, escalation management, and human-agent collaboration within enterprise agentic environments. By integrating oversight principles with operational mechanisms, the proposed framework provides a practical foundation for deploying AI agents that remain aligned with human intent while enabling controlled progression toward higher autonomy.

SpecMAGIC: Specification-Guided Multi-Task Generalization in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Multi-task generalization is an important yet underexplored problem in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). Many real-world applications require multi-agent systems to generalize across complex, temporally extended tasks with diverse inter-agent coordination requirements. We introduce a specification language that combines temporal-logic task specifications with operators for expressing such requirements. We propose SpecMAGIC, a hierarchical framework that leverages the automaton structure of task specifications for subtask decomposition. A centralized high-level planner identifies and allocates compatible subtask sequences across agents, while decentralized low-level policies execute individual subtasks. This enables zero-shot generalization to new tasks by composing reusable subtask policies according to new specifications, without retraining. We establish soundness and probabilistic completeness of the high-level planner. Experiments on multi-agent navigation tasks show that SpecMAGIC substantially outperforms existing approaches on unseen task specifications and maintains strong performance as the number of agents increases.

Secure Agentic AI and Multimodal Intelligence in 6G Communication Systems

Sixth-generation (6G) communication systems are expected to support immersive, intent-driven, and latency-critical services through tight integration of multimodal intelligence, edge computing, and autonomous network control. In this setting, agentic AI systems can reason over heterogeneous inputs, use tools and network functions, coordinate across distributed entities, and adapt to dynamic environments. These capabilities make agentic multimodal intelligence promising for 6G applications such as semantic communications, digital twins, and zero-touch network management, but they also introduce security and privacy risks that extend beyond conventional AI and network security models. Vulnerabilities can propagate across multimodal data pipelines, memory, tools, communication interfaces, and inter-agent coordination loops. We survey multimodal intelligence and agentic AI for 6G cross-layered communication systems from the perspectives of privacy and security. We present a 6G-oriented system view spanning perception, memory, tool use, and multi-agent orchestration; analyze threat models and attack surfaces across sensing, cognition, action, memory, and communication layers; organize existing attacks into a structured taxonomy; and review mitigation strategies including prompt and memory defenses, inter-agent trust mechanisms, secure orchestration, and privacy-preserving learning. We conclude by outlining open research directions for trustworthy multimodal multi-agent 6G systems.

Self-Assessment Reinforcement Learning for Search-State Awareness in Retrieval-Augmented Large Language Models

Recent advances in search agents have enabled large language models to tackle complex tasks by retrieving and synthesizing external information. However, existing search agents often lack search-state awareness, i.e., the ability to recognize what information has been obtained and what remains missing, leading to hallucinated reasoning and prematurely terminated search. To address this limitation, we propose Self-Assessment Reinforcement Learning (SARL), a reinforcement learning framework in which models learn to recognize their own search state during training and improve their search behavior accordingly, without requiring additional assessment at test time. SARL achieves this through a dual reward design that captures the corrective effect of each assessment while penalizing excessive reliance on it, jointly encouraging effective and efficient search trajectories. Under this setup, SARL achieves a 24.3% relative improvement over the strongest baseline, with average gains of 35.9% on multi-hop reasoning and 27.9% on deep research benchmarks against the strongest per-benchmark baselines. SARL further surpasses methods built on auxiliary supervision or multi-agent coordination, despite operating as a standalone RL framework with no inference-time scaffolding. Beyond final performance, SARL maintains training stability under extended search horizons, a regime where existing methods consistently degrade, enabling sustained gains as task complexity grows.

RoleFlow: Self-Evolving Multi-Role Agentic Collaboration via Structural Adaptation

Large language model (LLM) based multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their adaptability to evolving task requirements. Although recent approaches enable dynamic orchestration, trajectory-level rewards they apply assign one outcome to each decision, ofen struggling to identify the contributions of individual structural changes. We propose RoleFlow, a self-evolving multi-role agentic system that refines collaboration structures through feedback-driven atomic edits and on-demand role synthesis. To make structural adaptation learnable, we introduce Single-Edit Counterfactual Policy Optimization (SECPO), which evaluates each edit against its no-edit counterpart at the same chain state and assigns targeted credit to edit-specific tokens. Across seven benchmarks spanning four domains, RoleFlow achieves the optimal average performance, surpassing the strongest competitor by 4.7 points. It leads on all three multi-hop QA benchmarks by 3.2-7.5 F1 points and also shows supremacy on mathematical reasoning, web interaction, and code generation. Further analyses demonstrate cross-benchmark transferability, training-data efficiency, and cross-executor generalization.

High-Value Training Data from Noisy, Diverse Sources

Training state-of-the-art models increasingly requires learning from large, heterogeneous datasets whose examples vary in quality, domain relevance, and source of supervision. Dataweighting---assigning different weights to training examples---offers a general way to control how such data influences training, but learning effective weights at scale remains challenging. We develop a scalable approach to learning dataweights: given a small, high-quality target dataset, we use metagradients through the training process to efficiently optimize training-example weights directly for performance on the target domain. To scale beyond a fixed dataset and model, we learn parametric weighting rules that generalize to new and streaming examples and transfer from smaller models to substantially larger ones. We study reward modeling as a prototypical application, where preference data may be noisy, adversarial, synthetic, or mismatched with the target domain. Learned dataweights improve held-out performance, automatically adapt reward models to specialized domains, and allow mixtures of human and synthetic supervision to outperform either source alone.

Agent Mosaic: Recognizing Confidentiality Leaks in Agentic LLM Systems

Modern LLM agents can retrieve information from accessible documents, and then disclose a sensitive conclusion assembled from them. This mosaic effect creates a critical risk for agents operating over confidential data. We quantify its realization with 30 synthetic cybersecurity, medical, and military examples, yielding 10,800 final answers from three LLM agents in controlled simulation and 1,440 answers from live agents in two open-source harnesses. Even under benign queries designed to be answerable from one document, GPT-5.6-Sol, the best performing model tested, discloses forbidden composites in 7.08% of answers (95% CI: 3.75-10.67%) in simulation, with worse disclosure rates in live harnesses. Adding visible classification headers and explicit aggregation rules reduces pooled disclosure rates substantially, yet unsafe disclosures persist in 32.37% of simulated answers (95% CI: 28.33-36.19%) across all intents despite these safeguards. Furthermore, increased refusals do not guarantee confidentiality, since significant refusals reveal the prohibited conclusion regardless. These results point to a troubling conclusion: sensitive data leaks via mosaic-style events are an observed confidentiality failure mode in the evaluated settings at non-negligible rates. They motivate stronger controls on what agents may infer and disclose in sensitive data environments with mixed-clearance users.

AcoustiPrint: Physics-Guided Neural Design of Manufacturable Acoustic Structures

Recent work in additive manufacturing and neural inverse design enables the creation of complex three-dimensional structures to manipulate physical fields, yet these designs must also adhere to discrete, non-differentiable fabrication constraints. Current methods limit the design space by imposing differentiable soft manufacturability loss penalties, or by fixing structures through post-processing, causing a discrepancy between the geometry optimized by the physics model and the final fabricated result. Thus, we present AcoustiPrint, a physics-informed neural architecture for constrained inverse design that represents three-dimensional geometries with an implicit neural representation (INR) and passes each candidate through a printability operator before evaluating its final response. The printability operator executes layer-wise self-support, discrete material selection, and whole-body connectivity, assuring fabrication feasibility. To allow for backpropagation through the discrete non-differentiable operations, we use surrogate gradients for the backward pass and a self-supervised printability objective that progressively drives the INR toward a valid design domain. We demonstrate AcoustiPrint for personalized transcranial focused ultrasound, where inter-patient differences in acoustic properties and head geometry deform wave propagation and requires patient-specific acoustic focusing. AcoustiPrint jointly optimizes three-dimensional geometry and material composition through differentiable wave physics while ensuring manufacturability. Experiments show manufacturable acoustic structures with enhanced focal accuracy and over density-based and phase-based baselines, demonstrating a generalized methodology for neural design through non-differentiable manufacturing constraints and differentiable physics.

Soft Ontology Priors over Latent Concept Posteriors for Weakly Supervised Neuro-Symbolic Learning

Neuro-symbolic (NeSy) predictors that supervise only the task label suffer from reasoning shortcuts: distinct concept mappings achieve the same maximum likelihood, leaving the recovered concepts unidentifiable and unsafe for downstream reuse. We attack this failure by injecting a soft ontology prior over the latent concepts into the training objective and compiling it together with the task constraint into a smooth, decomposable probabilistic circuit. Under independent Bernoulli perception, the normalisers of the resulting structured concept posterior reduce to weighted model counts that are linear in the compiled circuit size, because the neural leaf weights fold into the circuit before evaluation. The soft compilation strategy places expert axioms in the circuit's weights rather than its support (task-structural axioms guaranteed by the data-generating process stay hard), so noisy expert axioms only redistribute prior mass and cannot empty the support. We characterise how the prior reweights label-restricted shortcut mass through differences in ontology violation energy, show that soft priors incur bounded log-density perturbations under bounded axiom noise, and prove that the hard-support counterpart can be made degenerate by a single corrupted axiom. We validate these predictions on an audited six-concept view of the real-world BDD-OIA driving benchmark and on controlled NeSy tasks.

MemPlan: Memory-Conditioned PDDL Planning for Partially Observable Text Environments

LLM agents are flexible in language interaction, but using them as long-horizon planners is costly and prone to hallucinated or invalid actions. This paper targets a different role for LLMs: constrained bridges between language and symbolic planning. We propose MemPlan, a memory-conditioned PDDL planning framework for partially observable interactive text environments. MemPlan keeps the PDDL domain fixed and reconstructs the PDDL problem at each step from parsed observations, candidate hypotheses, and interaction memory. Positive graph memory assigns relevance-based action costs to candidate hypotheses for missing objects, locations, or conditions, guiding the planner toward more plausible checks. Negative memory records execution-refuted assumptions and compiles them into exclusion constraints, preventing repeated attempts at candidates already contradicted by feedback. A unified schema-conditioned language--symbol interface, trained offline with supervised fine-tuning, connects text environments to the planning loop through observation-to-fact parsing and symbolic-action-to-command grounding. Experiments on TextWorld, ALFWorld, and Robotouille show that MemPlan improves task completion and step efficiency over language-only and PDDL-aware baselines, while substantially reducing online token cost compared with LLM-planning baselines such as ReAct. The same fine-tuned interface is also reused across TextWorld task types without task-specific fine-tuning. Our code and data are available at: https://github.com/tewiSong/MemPlan.

Probing and Steering for Safety-Relevant Behaviours in Large Language Models

Activation probes are computed directions in a language model's residual stream, used both to detect safety-relevant behaviours and, by adding their direction back into the stream, to steer them. We explore how the main design choices behind such probes interact, within one pipeline, on one model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct), and across three behaviours: deception, high-stakes awareness and sycophancy. The pipeline crosses two estimators (difference-of-means and logistic regression) with three token-pooling schemes (last, mean and learned attention), selects appropriate layers to detect or steer a behaviour, and evaluates every direction twice: as a detector of the behaviour, and as a steering vector to push outputs towards the behaviour. Prior work read together suggests a read/write dissociation, in which logistic regression reads (detects) best but difference-of-means directions write (steer) best. We find that it holds for deception and high-stakes but not for sycophancy, where the pattern reverses. The reversal appears to follow partially from the data rather than from the estimators. The best pooling technique and the layer choice depend on the behaviour and data, and the layer that steers best can sit well before the layer that detects best. The right steering metric to use between changing a model's perception and eliciting behaviour depends on the task - and the two can disagree outright. The exported probes and directions power an interactive interface through which a user can score and steer text directly.

Route-and-Generate: KL-Regularized Router Improvement for Reasoning Routing in LLMs

Large language models often apply complex reasoning indiscriminately, leading to overthinking on simple tasks and underthinking on harder ones. We present Route-and-Generate (Rouge), a hierarchical training framework that enables LLMs to adaptively decide when and how much reasoning is required for each input. Our approach decouples reasoning effort allocation from answer generation by introducing a routing policy that allocates a fixed sampling budget across multiple reasoning formats, while the base model is optimized with GRPO using per-sample rewards. To promote efficient reasoning, we employ a rank-based reward that favors shorter correct solutions, and update the routing policy via a closed-form KL-regularized policy improvement step distilled into a neural router. This design encourages concise answers on easy problems while reserving longer reasoning for harder ones, achieving improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs across multiple reasoning benchmarks.

Consistency Is Not Validity: Self-Rewarded Moral Argumentation Makes Language Models Decisive but Pliable

LLMs are increasingly consulted for moral judgment. We aim to improve performance on such tasks by rewarding good arguments, on the premise that arguing well increases the likelihood of judging well. We train a reinforcement learning policy initialized from a state-of-the-art language model in a self-judged tournament with masked thinking: the generated arguments are judged pairwise on argument quality by the policy itself. As an empirical basis, we extract authentic dilemmas from classic novels, using a logic solver to verify that each constitutes a conflict. We show that our judge rates argument quality like human annotators, and the policy then adopts the language register that it rewards. Testing on the external benchmarks MoralChoice, ELEPHANT, and MORABLES, we find that the policy becomes more decisive and invariant to the order in which options are presented, but sycophancy and reframing remain a challenge to validity. Two ablation studies with synthetic dilemmas and unmasked thinking match the reordering gains, but show a weaker register shift and higher sycophancy. We conclude that consistency is not validity: consistency under reordering can be optimized without improving the moral judgment it is meant to signal.

MPBRS: Multi-Potential-Based Reward Shaping for Unified Reward Configuration in Humanoid Locomotion

Designing and tuning reward functions remains one of the main challenges in building effective reinforcement learning (RL) pipelines for humanoid locomotion. While shaping rewards can accelerate learning, naively formulated terms often conflict with the desired behavior, overfit to a specific robot, or become unstable under small weight changes. Potential-Based Reward Shaping (PBRS) offers an alternative solution: shaping the reward via differences of a potential function to guide exploration without changing the optimal policy. In this paper, we study Multi-PBRS for humanoid locomotion and propose a unified reward configuration. We then evaluate the same reward structure and weights across nine humanoid robots, requiring only robot-specific metadata (joint/link mappings and default poses) rather than per-robot reward redesign. Our experiments show that, compared to standard shaping, MPBRS provides more consistent scaling behavior and reduced tuning sensitivity across robots, making it easier to tune without altering the optimal policy.

TerraTransfer++: Demonstration-Free End-to-End Planner

End-to-end autonomous driving has achieved state-of-the-art performance on benchmarks and real-world deployments. Its standard training recipe, however, is expensive across all existing paradigms: imitation learning requires fleet-scale driving logs whose behavior distribution only thinly covers the safety-critical tail; closed-loop reinforcement learning in a photorealistic simulator, while offering diverse behaviors, is bottlenecked by sample-efficiency, per-step cost of rendering, and a forward pass through a large vision backbone. Self-play in vectorized simulators changes the economics: at millions of rollout steps per second, one can obtain a state distribution with natural diversity in collisions, near-misses, and recoveries that no driving log contains, and a highly reactive policy emerges from a basic driving-quality reward. Our approach exploits this asymmetry. We pretrain a single policy by self-play, distill its rollouts into a flow-matching trajectory teacher that also plans, and train lightweight adapters on a frozen pretrained vision backbone to reproduce the latent space of either teacher through the action KL divergence, a batch-relational low-rank structural loss, and, for the planner, a trajectory-matching loss. Every target traces back to the self-play policy, so alignment never supervises against a logged trajectory: a paired dataset of (image, scene-state) frames suffices, with no need for the curated expert demonstrations that imitation pretraining is built on. From this one self-play teacher we obtain two end-to-end students: a closed-loop policy that outperforms every published imitation-based end-to-end method on the photorealistic 3D Gaussian splatting closed-loop benchmark HUGSim, and a planner that reaches 59.4 EPDMS on NAVSIM navhard, above every published method including DriveZero-Scale (57.1), and 95.5 PDMS on navtest, without human demonstrations.

Gender, Sexual Orientation, and the One-Year Refugee Bar: A Computational Study of Bill C-12 and Canadian Refugee Adjudication

In March 2026, Bill C-12 amended the Immigration and Refugee Protection Act to make refugee claims ineligible for referral to the Refugee Protection Division where they are made more than one year after the claimant's first entry to Canada. Affected claimants are diverted to a Pre-Removal Risk Assessment (PRRA), which is typically decided by an immigration officer on a paper record, with no guaranteed oral hearing, no appeal on the merits, and no automatic stay of removal pending judicial review. The government defended the bar partly on the premise that genuine refugees claim promptly. Critics countered that late claims are concentrated among vulnerable groups, particularly people fleeing persecution based on gender, sexual orientation, gender identity, or gender expression (SOGIE).
  This article tests those competing premises. Using artificial intelligence tools, checked through human review, it examines nearly 29,000 published decisions of the Refugee Protection Division, the Refugee Appeal Division, and the Federal Court from 2006 to 2025. The results of this study demonstrate that, first, while most claimants claim within a year, roughly one in nine in the published record does not, and gender and SOGIE claims are significantly overrepresented among these late claims. Second, among late claims, refusals of gender and SOGIE claims are more likely than refusals of other claims to be overturned on appeal or judicial review. Late claims on other grounds, by contrast, fare somewhat worse on review than timely claims. The one-year bar therefore removes the hearing, the appeal, and the stay from the late claimants for whom those safeguards have been doing the most work. The article recommends repealing the bar or, failing that, adopting exemptions, procedural protections, and greater transparency in PRRA decision making.

GAV: A scientific experimentation environment for PDE agents

Reliable feedback is essential for coding agents to develop programs for partial differential equations (PDEs). Yet evaluating these programs on new PDE problems often requires expensive reference computations, while fixed datasets restrict the range of experiments. We introduce Generator-Agent-Verifier (GAV), a scientific experimentation environment that makes new PDE problems verifiable by construction. The Generator learns a distribution of continuous reference solutions using data from numerical simulations or experimental measurements to represent a target physical regime. Using the method of manufactured solutions, it works backward from the generated reference solutions to construct matching PDE problems without a separate numerical solve. The Agent designs experiments, investigates failures, and iteratively improves its program. The Verifier evaluates candidate programs against the reference solutions and provides numerical feedback on accuracy and computational cost. This feedback can also guide the generation of new problems that probe the current program's weaknesses. We provide a theoretical analysis of evaluation reliability and guided development. Experiments on Navier-Stokes and Vlasov-Poisson-type equations across different data sources and regimes demonstrate the effectiveness of GAV. In particular, on the numerical Navier-Stokes task, GAV reduces the mean test error of three existing PDE agents by more than an order of magnitude relative to their original setups. Code is available at https://github.com/PotatoJnny/PDE-GAV.

Generalizing Metropolis-Hastings for DAG Sampling

A fundamental challenge in Bayesian network structure learning lies in efficiently approximating the posterior distribution of directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). For large, high-dimensional datasets, the task remains beyond the capabilities of current methods. This work advances Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms by introducing novel moves that simultaneously resample the parents for multiple nodes of the DAG. At the core of these moves is duet sampling, a new generalization of the Metropolis-Hastings algorithm that selects two proposal distributions from a pool at each iteration. One is used for generating a candidate state of the Markov chain and another for evaluating the return to the current state; both are ingredients of an appropriately crafted acceptance criterion. Pairing different proposal distributions provides additional freedom and enables constructing Markov chains that mix more efficiently than their basic Metropolis-Hastings counterparts, at no additional computational cost. Experiments show that our new moves, both individually and in combination, outperform the previous state of the art, which relies on a more restrictive pool of moves. This advantage persists even when letting the previous methods leverage duet sampling to enhance their performance.
